#2953. IRS unveils higher capital gains tax brackets for 2027

The IRS has unveiled higher capital gains tax brackets for 2027.

In its announcement Thursday, the agency boosted the taxable income limits for the long-term capital gains brackets, which apply to assets owned for more than one year.

It also increased figures for dozens of other provisions, including federal income tax brackets, the estate and gift tax exemption, and eligibility for the earned income tax credit, among others.

Despite post-election stock market volatility, many long-term investors hold profitable assets after years of market growth.

The new capital gains brackets could impact investors' 2027 plans for sales or rebalancing, which can trigger taxes for brokerage account investments.

The latest round of IRS inflation adjustments for 2027 come as many Americans wrestle with affordability amid the rising cost of housing, groceries, gasoline and other living expenses.

The capital gains rate you pay — 0%, 15% or 20% — is based on which bracket you fall into based on taxable income.

You calculate taxable income by subtracting the greater of the standard or itemized deductions from your adjusted gross income.

For 2027, the standard deduction will rise to $16,600 for single filers, up from $16,100, and $33,200 for married couples filing jointly, up from $32,200.

Starting in 2027, single filers with taxable income of $51,050 or less and married couples filing jointly with $102,100 or less, will qualify for the 0% long-term capital gains rate.

#2951. Has Danny Wolf’s breakout game unlocked frontcourt potential?

The Nets cried Wolf. This time, he was telling the truth.

Despite selecting a historic slew of first-round picks in 2025, the Nets had seen just two of those rookies, Egor Demin and Nolan Traore, crack Jordi Fernández's rotation through the first seven games.

Injuries mounted alongside Brooklyn's losses. Entering Wednesday night with a 1-6 record and coming off a deflating loss to the rival Knicks across the river, they were still winless on their home floor. Urgency kicked in.

Noah Clowney suffered a knee injury in the first half and was ruled out for the remainder of the night, joining Demin, Joshua Jefferson, Ben Saraf, and Drake Powell on the inactive list — five rookie-contract players forced to watch from the sidelines (or Long Island).

Before Wednesday, the former 27th pick had logged minutes in just two games. His 27 minutes on the court was less than two-way's Grant Nelson's 35.

But by the second half, he anchored a unit that turned a tight game into an uncontested 114–104 victory, finishing with 13 points, five rebounds, and three assists on 4-of-6 shooting — including two 3-pointers and a game-high +17 in just under 19 minutes.

"Obviously in the NBA, there's going to be times especially early in your career that you might not be in the rotation, and that was the case for me starting the year," Wolf admitted postgame. "Everyone emphasizes and preaches 'you got to be ready' and you hear it over and over… When coach called my name in the second half, I was ready."

It's a small sample size, but when Wolf knocks down shots and makes plays as a secondary facilitator, he gives Brooklyn a distinct way to generate offense in transition. That dynamic starts on the glass: the more defensive stops the Nets string together, the more opportunities Wolf has to snag a rebound and push the pace before opposing defenses can set up.

General manager Sean Marks has long shown an appreciation for frontcourt players who can rebound and push — from Trevor Booker to Ben Simmons to Julius Randle. Wolf fits that modern "grab-and-go" archetype, but adds a legit 3-point stroke to the equation.

"The biggest staple of our team, especially our offensive identity, is supposed to be playing fast," Wolf explained postgame. "We use the terminology 'pace and space' and I feel like there's times with my play style that I can really help us push the ball up the floor, make simple passes, start the break. Momentum is what helps win you a basketball game."

The sequence that swung Wednesday night's game highlighted that approach. Moments after checking in during the third quarter, Wolf snagged a defensive rebound, pushed the pace, and initiated a transition drive that resulted in a Julius Randle AND-1 layup. On the next possession, he trailed the play, spotted up behind the arc, and hit a 24-foot triple to give Brooklyn its first lead of the second half at 61–59, forcing a Charlotte timeout.

To make it work, Fernández sat three-time All-Star Julius Randle down the stretch in favor of the rookie. Crucially, Fernández didn't deploy Wolf as a standalone center.

Wolf played every single minute alongside a traditional interior anchor — logging 15 minutes and 37 seconds next to Day'Ron Sharpe and 2 minutes and 59 seconds alongside Moritz Wagner.

Protecting Wolf from heavy low-post drop coverage and rim-protection duties allowed him to roam freely and make decisions in the open floor. During Wolf's 15:37 alongside Sharpe, the Nets outscored Charlotte 46-32 (+14), with Wolf racking up 10 of his 13 points, all five of his rebounds, and two of his three assists.

The framework for utilizing a player with Wolf's skillset is familiar to Fernández from his time under Michael Malone in Denver.

"I think that you have to be creative with players that are uncommon," Fernández said in Oct. 2025. "I was very fortunate to work with Nikola [Jokic], and we had to let him show us what he was able to do. Otherwise, if you see him as a traditional player, then you could fail… Nikola showed certain things that were so different for his position and a seven-footer, and that's why he makes all these plays and plays in situations where no other big man plays."

It isn't about becoming the next Jokic, though. Wolf finding his footing in the NBA has required him adjusting from his heavy-usage roles at Yale and Michigan, where his playmaking came from high volume.

"I think that the coaches [at Michigan] didn't even know what my role was going to be," Wolf also said in Oct. 2025. "At Yale, I was more of a traditional 5, and then at Michigan, I don't even know what you would call my position or how I played… In the NBA, it's about continuing to get better within the confines of the roles your coach wants you to play."

On Wednesday, that work in progress delivered real results.

Wolf opened the fourth quarter by hitting a 25-foot transition jumper off a pass from Terance Mann. Operating out of the high-post, he delivered a crisp pass to Traore for a driving floater before driving into the lane himself for an And-1 layup. When Charlotte made a late push, Wolf secured a key offensive rebound, drew the foul, and sank both free throws to seal the victory.

Michael Porter Jr. echoed that praise in the locker room.

"I ain't gonna lie, the difference tonight was Terance Mann and Danny Wolf. They haven't been in rotation much, they came in ready to play… Danny coming in and contributing right away like that, that takes a lot of readiness and a lot of discipline to not play for so many games, come in the second half, and contribute the way he did. I always thought Danny was a special player, and I've said that since he got here."

During his first Summer League in August 2025, Wolf said he's a "work in progress." Now, some of the progress is tangible and helping lead to wins.

#2949. IRS announces new federal income tax brackets for 2027

The IRS has announced new federal income tax brackets and standard deductions for 2027.

In its announcement Thursday, the agency raised the income thresholds for each bracket, which apply to tax year 2027 for returns filed in 2028.

The IRS also increased figures for other key provisions, including long-term capital gains brackets, the estate and gift tax exemption, and eligibility for the earned income tax credit, among others.

For 2027, the top rate of 37% applies to individuals with taxable income above $661,375 and married couples filing jointly earning $793,650 or more.

Federal income tax brackets show how much you owe on each part of your taxable income, which you calculate by subtracting the greater of the standard or itemized deductions from your adjusted gross income.

The standard deduction will also increase in 2027, rising to $33,200 for married couples filing jointly, up from $32,200 in 2026. Starting in 2027, single filers can claim $16,600, a bump up from $16,100.

The new tax brackets come as Americans wrestle with affordability issues amid the rising cost of housing, groceries, gasoline and other living expenses.

The consumer price index climbed 3.7% in September 2026 from one year earlier, up from a yearly pace of 3.4% in August, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported in October.

The U.S. federal income tax system is progressive, which means tax rates increase as earnings rise.

For 2027, the federal brackets include seven income tiers, with rates ranging from 10% to 37%. The tax rates apply to each band of income rather than a flat percentage for all earnings.

The IRS adjusts the income ranges for each tax rate yearly based on the consumer price index.

Your federal tax brackets depend on your filing status, which you choose on your tax return. The options include single, married filing jointly, married filing separately, head of household or qualifying surviving spouse.

You can find your 2025 taxable income on line 15 of your individual federal income tax return, known as Form 1040.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

#2945. IRS announces bigger estate and gift tax exemption for 2027

The Internal Revenue Service has announced a higher estate and gift tax exemption for 2027.

In its announcement Thursday, the agency increased the federal "basic exclusion amount" to $15.48 million per person for 2027, up from $15 million in 2026. The exemption applies to tax-free transfers during life and at death.

State estate and inheritance tax exemptions can be lower.

The IRS also increased figures for dozens of other provisions, including federal income tax brackets, the capital gains tax brackets and eligibility for the earned income tax credit, among others.

The inflation adjustments for 2027 come as Americans wrestle with affordability issues amid the rising cost of housing, groceries, gasoline and other living expenses.

President Donald Trump's "big beautiful bill" increased the estate and gift tax exemption to $15 million per person. Without the change from Congress, the exemption would have reverted to 2017 levels — $5 million plus an inflation adjustment — after 2025, which spurred proactive estate planning from ultra-wealthy families before the legislation.

Under current law, few families are subject to federal estate taxes. For 2026, families will file about 6,890 federal estate tax returns, and roughly 3,900 will be taxable, according to estimates from the Tax Policy Center, a think tank.

#2943. Mississippi Senate: Colom, Hyde-Smith gird for ballot legal fight in razor-thin race

An ultra-tight Mississippi Senate race between Republican incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith and Democratic challenger Scott Colom could end with one of the biggest shocks in a midterm election cycle already chock full of stunning defeats of GOP candidates.

With Colom in the lead by just 1,813 votes on Thursday, Hyde-Smith's campaign set the stage for a potential legal battle over ballots by demanding in a letter to county commissioners and circuit clerks in all 82 Mississippi counties that they immediately separate all provisional ballots cast in the election from regular and absentee ballots.

"These measures are necessary to ensure that each affidavit ballot's eligibility for counting andultimate disposition can be verified," the letter said.

Lawyers for Colom's campaign responded with a letter of their own to the counties, calling the Republican's demands "unlawful and extraordinary measures."

"Those demands have no basis in Mississippi law, and if you depart from ordinary election procedures as you have been asked to do, you will violate Mississippi voters' rights and Mississippi election law," they said.

Provisional ballots, which are also known as affidavit ballots, are cast by a voter whose name does not appear on the poll book, who does not present an acceptable photo ID, or in several other situations.

The ballot is then placed in a sealed envelope and later examined by election officials to see if it can be cast, or the voter can return with a photo ID or evidence within five business days to show they are entitled to cast the ballot.

When the letters were sent, Colom had 49.04% of the counted ballots, compared to 48.85% for Hyde-Smith, with an estimated more than 95% of the votes in, according to the Associated Press.

Colom's lead ticked up slightly late Thursday afternoon to 1,813 votes out of a total of 951,744 counted as of 6:17 p.m. ET, the AP said. Ty Pinkins, an independent candidate, had 20,007 votes, or 2.1% of the total count.

A final result may not be available until next week because more ballots can still arrive and be counted.

Mississippi's election procedures do not have an automatic recount provision.

"Absolutely, I didn't think it would be this close," said Brian Anderson, a political science professor at the Mississippi University for Women in Columbus.

Anderson said that before the election, he thought Colom, a 43-year-old district attorney, might get into position to lose by just 3 or 4 percentage points, which would have been a very solid showing in a state that hasn't elected a Democrat to the Senate since 1982.

"I'm much more surprised" now that Colom has a chance to win, Anderson said.

If Colom defeats Hyde-Smith, 67, he would be the first Black senator from Mississippi since the post-Civil War Reconstruction Era.

"The Democrats have struggled here to create a pipeline of candidates," Anderson said. A Colom win "would galvanize the Democratic Party in a way it has not been," he said.

Neither candidate's campaign immediately responded to requests for comment from CNBC.

Hyde-Smith's X account, in a post Thursday afternoon, said, "Cindy Hyde-Smith is going to represent Mississippi in the United States Senate for the next six years."

"We believe thousands of votes remain to be counted from dozens of Republican counties, and we are confident those votes will reinforce her path to victory," the post said. "Election officials are continuing the normal process of counting outstanding ballots, including absentee ballots. They must have the time to complete that work and ensure every lawful ballot is accurately counted and every Mississippian's voice is heard."

The post added, "We expect Democratic lawyers to pull out every stop, and we are prepared. Our focus is on completing the count and returning Cindy Hyde-Smith to the Senate to keep fighting for Mississippi values."

"Mississippi is the firewall, and this race is our line in the sand," the message said.

In a pair of ironic twists, if Colom wins, his victory could be attributed in some ways to actions taken by two Republicans: President Donald Trump and Hyde-Smith herself.

Supreme Court Justice Amy Coney Barrett, whom Trump appointed, wrote the court's 5-4 majority ruling in June that upheld a Mississippi law allowing mail-in absentee ballots postmarked by Election Day to be counted if they are received within five business days.

Those ballots could provide Colom a margin of victory. There are also an estimated 10,000 to 15,000 provisional ballots that require verification of eligibility to vote.

A lawyer for the Republican National Committee, whose lawsuit argued federal law barred counting votes received after Election Day, noted in a brief to the Supreme Court, "Democratic voters tend to mail their ballots later on average than Republican voters, which results in late-arriving ballots favoring Democratic candidates."

Trump at the time of the Supreme Court ruling called it "a tremendous loss" for voters' rights.

Hyde-Smith was appointed to her Senate seat in 2018 after the resignation of Thad Cochran and later that year won a special election for the seat. She was elected to a full term in 2020.

In 2023, she effectively blocked Colom's nomination by then-President Joe Biden to a post as a federal judge for the Northern District of Mississippi. Colom's nomination had the support of Mississippi's other Republican senator, Roger Wicker, and two former GOP governors.

Hyde-Smith said she opposed Colom's nomination for reasons that included his purported support for transgender students participating in girls' sports and because his campaign for district attorney had allegedly received money from billionaire George Soros.

Colom, in an April 10, 2023, letter to Hyde-Smith, said he was unaware before his hometown newspaper reported it that Soros had donated to a political action committee that had supported his campaign. He also wrote that he was not sure why Hyde-Smith had suggested he "opposed legislation to protect female athletes."

Colom concluded the letter by promising to be guided by the law, not his personal views on public policy, if he were confirmed as a judge.

"I hope you will reconsider your views about my nomination," he wrote.

Hyde-Smith did not reconsider her views and maintained her block on the nomination.

Colom announced his candidacy for the Democratic nomination for Senate in September 2025 and recently told CNN, "Me not having a federal judgeship is not a motivating factor for me at all" in running.

Anderson said Hyde-Smith ran "a low-visibility campaign," at least compared to Colom's, and that she focused on voters who traditionally would be expected to support her.

Hyde-Smith refused to debate Colom several times.

Colom "knows how to speak to people's interests, and Hyde-Smith was all about values, which is great for a lot of people, but it's all about the interests right now," Anderson said.

Those interests include concerns about affordability, the Iran war and corruption.

Colom is "bringing up all those," Anderson said.

Anderson noted that Colom's campaign had pounced on a remark Hyde-Smith made during a Feb. 22 interview with a local television outlet.

In the interview, Hyde-Smith answered a question about rising beef prices by saying, "You know, there so many proteins to choose from."

Colom's campaign featured a video clip of that remark in an ad featuring people reacting incredulously to Hyde-Smith's suggestion.

"Pick another protein?" one woman said in the ad. "She done lost her d--- mind. I'd rather pick another senator."

#2942. Vikings-Bills First Injury Report

The Minnesota Vikings and Buffalo Bills released their first injury reports in advance of their Monday Night Football matchup at U.S. Bank Stadium. Here they are:

PUP = Physically unable to participate list.

IR = Injured reserve. Note: only starters on IR are listed.

Probable = the league defines a Probable injury designation as a 75% or greater chance of playing. Historically, 95% of players designated probable are active and played on game day.

Questionable = the league defines a Questionable injury designation as being 'uncertain' of whether the player will play or not. This replaces the former definition of having a 50% chance of playing. Historically, 70-75% of players with a Questionable injury designation were active and played on game day.

Doubtful = The league defines a Doubtful injury designation to mean the player is unlikely to participate in the game. In practical terms, teams use the designation when a player has less than a 50% chance to suit up, and active game appearances from this designation remain rare. Historically only around 6% of players with a Doubtful designation are active and played on game day.

Out = will not play. Players with this designation are not active on game day and league rules prohibit them from playing.

For the Vikings, Kyler Murray being out is the big news and he will not play on Monday night. Theo Jackson also did not practice on Thursday and seems more questionable whether he'll play or not. Kevin O'Connell said they have other safeties like Jay Ward who could play in Jackson's absence and a few others are possbilities to be active on Monday night if need be as well.

The rest of the injuries appear to be more of the 'playing through it' variety and don't seem like they'll keep those players out on Monday night.

For the Bills, a laundry list of players on the injury report including three offensive line starters. Starting left tackle Dion Dawkins did not practice Thursday with a rib injury, nor did starting guard Alec Andreesen, with an ankle injury. Right tackle Spencer Brown was limited with a back issue.

Backup wideout Joshua Palmer also missed Thursday's practice with a hamstring injury.

Other than that, the rest of the players on the list appear to be more of the playing through minor injuries type, except backups Tyrell Shavers and Jordan Hancock who have their windows open to come back from the PUP list and IR, respectively.

We'll see how it plays out with injury updates tomorrow and Saturday.

#2941. Did Miami finally start to find its defensive identity?

INGLEWOOD, Calif. — "The guys earned their ice. We haven't really earned our ice many times."

That's how Heat coach Erik Spoelstra described his team's effort in a 117-116 win over the Clippers Wednesday night. As the Heat stumbled out of the gate to a 2-5 start this season, the headlines focused on Giannis Antetokounmpo and Bam Adebayo playing next to each other more than off each other, and on how the offense looked clunky. Spoelstra knew that wasn't the issue, it was the bottom-five defense in the league that was keeping the Heat from finding its identity and winning games.

"You have to make plays defensively, and that's what we've been trying to do is find our identity," Spoelstra said. "The offensive stuff like that's way overblown. You know, going into this game, we have almost a top 10 ranked offense [11th in net rating]. That's ahead of where I thought it would be at this time. But our defense is way behind. That's why we lost games."

For 29 minutes Wednesday night in Los Angeles, from the start of the third quarter through Klay Thompson's overtime game-winner, the Miami Heat found that identity.

Miami used its length and athleticism to pressure the Clippers' ballhandlers up the court, switched pick-and-rolls more — and more effectively — than they have all season, and played bully ball against a team light on shot creation. Davion Mitchell was ripping the ball away from Darius Garland.

They looked like the team Spoelstra envisioned — a team that played intense, high-energy defense and would get enough offense from its stars. A team with a defense-first, gritty identity and turned that defense into offense.

"I think we turned it up in the second half, majorly," Thompson said. "That ball pressure at half court that Bam, Giannis, Davion, Pella [Larsson], Ryan [Conwell] had was just a game changer."

"One game, does that solve everything? No, but at least now we can see," Spoelstra said. "All right, this is what the plan was the first day of training camp. Everybody wrapped their mind around it, and it just hasn't gone according to plan. But you could see the efforts. The guys earned their ice."

They earned that ice in the second half — the second quarter was more of what had the Heat losing games to start the season. The Clippers went on a 30-4 run to open the frame and dropped a 43-point second quarter on the Heat, with Garland carving them up as LA shot 16-of-22 in the quarter, including hitting 6-of-7 in the paint and 8-of-13 from 3. The Clippers had a 172 offensive rating in the second quarter as they got the shots they wanted — 75% of their first half shots came at the rim or from 3 (and they were hitting those deep balls).

"We gave up 67 points in the first half, and we just really wanted to try to prove to ourselves that we could do something tough," Spoelstra said. "And we opened up the third quarter, had a really good defensive quarter, held them to 20 points, and then did it again, held them to 20 points in the fourth before overtime."

Miami did it for a night, but now comes the hard part: Doing it again. Especially on Thursday night, going up to the Bay Area for a back-to-back against a Warriors team finding their identity and with a pretty good shooter of their own in the backcourt.

"That's our identity. That's what we have to be. Not every other night. We gotta be there every night," Adabayo said of the second-half defensive effort. "We're not scoring, we gotta make sure they don't score either... Being disruptive, we were flying around. That's what it takes... It's gonna be moments when it's uncomfortable. So being able to get out of that comfort zone and then communicate, you see what the potential is."

Miami now has to live up to that defensive potential nightly — but the second half against the Clippers showed them the path.

#2940. Top Democrats call to rein in AI buildout after midterms sweep: 'National data center moratorium now'

After a big win in the U.S. midterm elections on Tuesday, some Democrats are already signaling they want to take on the artificial build-out, specifically data centers.

In a post on X, Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., touted referendums across the U.S. on Tuesday where voters in localities approved restrictions on data centers. Sanders, who caucuses with the Democrats, said the message to political leaders is clear.

"National data center moratorium now," he wrote in the post. "Americans don't want Big Tech oligarchs wrecking the environment, gutting jobs and threatening humanity's future."

In another post on X, Rep. Yassamin Ansari, D-Ariz., wrote that pausing AI development needs to be a top priority for the large Democratic majority in the House on Day 1. Rep. Greg Casar, D-Texas, the chair of the Congressional Progressive Caucus, amplified the post and said, "Yes!"

According to the CNBC Voter Poll, 56% of voters said AI and data centers were a crucial or major factor in their decision at the ballot box. Of those voters, 65% said they cast a ballot for a Democratic candidate for Congress.

Stocks linked to the artificial intelligence build-out declined slightly on Wednesday in the wake of the election, but the selling intensified on Thursday as investors worried Democratic wins could have a real impact on data center construction. Chip stocks led the decline. The iShares Semiconductor ETF (SOXX) tumbled nearly 9% on Tuesday. Nvidia declined 8%, while Advanced Micro Devices sunk more than 14%.

Democrats who flipped critical races on Tuesday also singled out big tech companies and data centers.

South Carolina Democrat Annie Andrews, who flipped a Senate seat in the state on Tuesday, launched a website during the campaign that branded her opponent — Sen. Darline Graham — "data center Darline."

In her victory speech on Tuesday, Andrews said voters chose a South Carolina "where decisions about communities are made by the people who live in our communities, not the billionaires or data center companies in Washington D.C."

Ohio Democrat Sherrod Brown, who is heading back to the Senate after defeating incumbent Republican Sen. Jon Husted on Tuesday, said in his victory speech, "I do not work for data centers."

Democratic governors also attacked data centers in their victory speeches. Pennsylvania Gov. Josh Shapiro, who signed an executive order in August to slow data center development, said big tech companies shouldn't determine residents' futures. "Instead, the folks in our local communities get the final say," he said.

Perhaps most important to the data center build-out is Texas, which is home to 27% of planned data center capacity, according to Wells Fargo.

Gina Hinojosa, the Democrat who ousted incumbent Republican Gov. Greg Abbott, didn't address data centers directly in her victory speech, though they played a key role in her campaign.

"When I am governor, I will issue a moratorium, a ban, on all data center approvals until laws can be passed by the legislature to protect Texans," she said in a video posted to her X in late October. "This is about one fight. Is this going to be a Texas that is by and for the people, or is this the billionaire's world and we just live here? Data centers are part of that fight."

#2939. NY Giants cut QB Jake Haener, work out four veterans

The New York Giants have cut backup quarterback Jake Haener on Thursday.

Haener, 27, joined the Giants late in the summer following Brandon Allen's poor performance in the preseason. The two battled to be the Giants third quarterback behind Jaxson Dart and Jameis Winston, and the Giants ultimately chose Haener.

Haener was cut in the Giants' final cuts down to their initial 53-man roster, but was signed to their practice squad. He served as the Giants' primary backup behind Jameis Winston immediately after Jaxson Dart's injury and when J.J. McCarthy was still learning the Giants' offense. He's since reverted to being the Giants' 3rd quarterback once McCarthy was elevated to the starting job.

Haener will likely be signed to the Giants' practice squad.

The move to cut Haener opens a roster spot which could be used by RB Eric Gray or WR Jalin Hyatt following the recent hand injury suffered by returner/receiver Charlie Jones. Jones was seen trying to field punts one-handed in practice, which reportedly didn't go well.

The Giants also worked out a quartet of veterans on Thursday.

WR/KR Steven Sims – Sims is a wide receiver and kick returner who has played for the Washignton Commanders (2019-2020), Buffalo Bills (2021 – offseason), Pittsburgh Steelers (2021-2022), Houston Texans (2023-2024), Baltimore Ravens (2024), Seattle Seahawks (2025 – offseason), Arizona Cardinals (2025)

WR/KR Maurice Alexander – Alexander is a former undrafted free agent out of Florida International who has played for the USFL Philadelphia Stars (2022), Detroit Lions (2022 – 2024), Chicago Bears (2025)

WR River Cracraft – Cracraft is a 32-year old veteran receiver with return experience. He was an All Pac-12 player who went undrafted due to a knee injury. He's played for the Denver Broncos (2018-2019), Philadelphia Eagles (2019 – practice squad), San Francisco 49ers (2020-2021), Miami Dolphins (2022-2024), Seattle Seahawks (2025 – offseason), Washington Commanders (2025)

P Wes Pahl – Pahl, 24, played for Western Kenucky and Oklahoma State over the last four years, averaging 46.1 yards per punt on 116 punts in 52 collegiate games. He was signed by the Cleveland Browns and recently released from their practice squad.

#2936. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2934. The Lakers are ‘surfing the wave’ of Luka Dončić’s crazy scoring run right now

It's like déjà vu all over again: Luka Dončić scored at least 40 points and the Los Angeles Lakers rode a vibrant offensive attack to victory.

For the fifth time in six games, Dončić eclipsed the 40-point barrier during Los Angeles' 116-103 win over the Sacramento Kings Wednesday night. The one "hiccup" amid this stretch was a 37-point outing against the Golden State Warriors on Friday. Over that span, the Slovenian is averaging 42.8 points, 8.5 rebounds, 8.3 assists and 1.8 steals while shooting 63.3% on 2-pointers, 44.9% beyond the arc (14.8 attempts per game!) and 89.7% at the charity stripe.

He'll rainbow home deep stepback triples, massage his way into crafty, feathery touch shots, draw tons of fouls and still prime teammates for easy shots with one-of-a-kind passing. Every Lakers game is a spectacle hosted by Dončić — and the supporting cast knows it.

"You can call it a wave, you can call it a tsunami, you can call it whatever you want," Walker Kessler told reporters on Wednesday following the Lakers' third straight win. "He's scoring the ball. If you want to call it a wave, we're surfing the wave a little bit when he's making those shots. I mean he's incredible."

With 308 points through eight games, Dončić became the first player since Michael Jordan (303 points) in 1988 to tally 300-plus in the first eight games of the season, according to NBA Stats. He also recorded his sixth straight game with at least 35 points, five rebounds and five assists to pass Damian Lillard for the longest such streak in league history.

Each night, he seizes control of the action like few others can. In fact, nobody else around the Association can do it like him right now. He is otherworldly dominant and continues etching his name into more rarified company.

"You felt it like in that second quarter," head coach JJ Redick said postgame Wednesday. "I thought, again, we were generating good looks and then he comes down and he makes like three shots in a row that there's five guys in the NBA can make and all of a sudden the tie ball game is a nine-, 10-point lead. The team feeds off that, too, when he has it going like that."

You can follow Jackson on Twitter at @jackfrank_jjf.

#2933. Fantasy Football Rankings: Hayden Winks' QB/TE/DEF/K Blueprint for Week 9

The Blueprint is my weekly column and basically everything that I use to build out the most accurate rankings I can. There will be an RB column, a WR column and this combination column on the onesie positions. You will find film notes and endless charts. Maybe enough charts to stress test the Yahoo website. I kid.

It's impossible to get every ranking thoroughly early in the week, so be sure to keep checking in on these columns through Sunday morning. As always, my full rankings at every position are available.

Burrow has been dealing as the season-long QB2 (26.2 PPG), generating +0.23 EPA per dropback (QB5). He is lighting up box scores through the air, leading the league in passing yards per game (302) and passing touchdowns per game (2.9). Cincinnati is leaning all the way into his arm, operating with a league-high 66% neutral pass rate (first).

That aggressive approach meets an Atlanta defense allowing 16.3 half PPR points per game to quarterbacks (23rd), where recent signal-callers like Tyler Huntley (24.0 points) and Caleb Williams (16.5 points) have put up functional days. The Bengals head in as 3-point road favorites with an implied team total of 27.25 points (sixth).

We'll see if Mike Evans (Achilles) can return, but it doesn't sound like Deebo Samuel Sr. will be back.

Purdy has been surgical as fantasy's QB4 (21.7 PPG), leading all qualified quarterbacks with +0.31 EPA per dropback (QB1). Over his last four starts against quality opponents, his efficiency has cooled to +0.03 EPA per dropback (16th) while averaging 17.3 fantasy points per game. His Week 7 outing without his starting receivers was his worst game of the year (16.0).

He draws a dream matchup against a Raiders defense that's allowing the fourth-most half PPR points per game to QBs (21.8). Las Vegas sits at the top right of the intersection between success rate and explosive rate allowed on dropbacks alongside the worst defenses in the NFL. It's allowed four straight QBs to finish as top-12 QBs. The 49ers enter as 9.5-point home favorites with a slate-leading implied team total of 30.5 points (first).

Allen's 28.2 fantasy points per game is currently ahead of the all-time fantasy record at QB (Lamar Jackson, 2024). He's done so without DJ Moore for half of the season. Moore was used properly last week with unlimited run-away routes, and then Dalton Kincaid found space downfield in multi-TE sets. It's a great formula.

The Vikings have been by far the best fantasy QB defense, however, allowing just 10.4 fantasy points per game. That's insane. Truly. They haven't faced an elite dual-threat QB this season, though.

Allen will be blitzed at a career-high rate, most likely. He's the QB5 in EPA per dropback against the blitz (+0.07), but that is far lower than on non-blitzes (+0.21, QB1). When blitzed, Allen has a 3.4% scramble rate compared to a 9% scramble rate when not blitzed. It's possible the Bills run a bit more often because of the pressure, as opposing offenses to Minnesota only pass 45% of the time in neutral situations (31st).

Buffalo enters as 3.5-point road favorites carrying an implied team total of 25.0 points (11th). It would make sense for Allen's fantasy output to take a hit here.

Mahomes was quite good last week considering the circumstances, finishing as the QB5 against a healthy Broncos defense. Across the year, Mahomes is the QB14 on QB12 EPA per dropback. He was slowed down by the Seahawks without Rashee Rice in Week 7, but the Chiefs have been one of the best offenses in the NFL when not facing a top-three defense this year. Their weaknesses at LT and WR are hidden best against mediocre opponents.

Insert the Jets, who may not have their top-two defensive linemen here. The Jets just allowed top-five fantasy outings from Kirk Cousins, Malik Willis and Drake Maye in their last three outings. It's obviously a great spot here. Kansas City enters as 9.5-point home favorites with a 28.75-point implied team total (third).

Williams is simply way more accurate this year. He's the QB2 in completion percentage over expected when adjusting for his throw depth. He's middle of the pack in sack rate (7.6%). And he's the QB4 in interception rate. Williams is seeing it extremely well, despite already facing the Vikings, Seahawks, Patriots and the feisty Falcons defenses. He's the QB3 in EPA per dropback, and he'll start spiking fantasy outings once he's pushed by the opponent.

The Bucs defense will be another tough defense to beat. They're fifth-best against fantasy QBs, and from Week 3 on, they've been the best defense in EPA per play, allowing just 15.8 points per game. They slowed down Dak Prescott in Week 5, then completely shut down Bryce Young and Michael Penix Jr. in the past two weeks. They have better pass-rushing depth and have enjoyed full breakouts from CBs Zyon McCollum and especially Jacob Parrish. Tampa Bay also wants to grind out the clock with a run game that is breaking my brain. Chicago returns home as 5.5-point home favorites sporting a 26.5-point implied team total (seventh).

The Goff outlier dud happened last week, finishing as the QB21 against the Vikings' chaotic defense. Despite that, Goff is the QB4 in EPA per dropback and pacing for 4,770 yards with 36 passing touchdowns. He's the QB6 in fantasy despite not having anything to show with his legs.

The Week 9 environment is pristine against a Miami defense allowing 20.8 half PPR points per game to QBs (sixth). Opposing quarterbacks have moved the ball against the Dolphins regularly, including recent outings from Joe Burrow (24.6 points), Geno Smith (23.5 points) and Drake Maye (22.4 points). Miami is struggling on play action and throws over the middle. That's the entire Lions offense. Detroit is listed as 7-point road favorites with a 28.75-point implied team total (third).

After a very slow start, Maye is the QB2 from Week 4 on, sandwiched between Joe Burrow and Josh Allen. This past month, his +0.39 EPA per dropback leads the position. He's been the QB14 in EPA per dropback throughout the season, but timely scrambles and plenty of volume have made up for the efficiency decline. We'll see if A.J. Brown and Hunter Henry can suit up later, but Maye made it work with backups against the Dolphins last week (QB7).

He now faces a Packers defense that has kept QBs in check with a chaotic defensive style, surrendering just 14.9 half PPR points per game to the position (27th). Green Bay recently held Bryce Young to 12.4 points, Jared Goff to 18.0 and Dak Prescott to 15.0. The Patriots head into the matchup as 3-point home favorites with an implied team total of 25.25 points (10th).

It hasn't been the best Prescott season, and the Cowboys' run game has been so bad that they've lost some inexcusable games along the way. The good news is Prescott has healthy WR and OL units right now, and his defense looks as pathetic as usual. He's been the QB13 in fantasy points per game this year on just QB8 EPA per dropback. It's been much higher than that for long stretches, and I can see that coming back after a recent stretch of games against BAL, HOU, TB and GB. Prescott was the QB6 and QB11 in the last two games against softer opponents.

The Colts defense is one of the worst in the NFL, possibly the worst without DT DeForest Buckner. They were smoked by the Jaguars last week after firing their GM in late October. Dallas heads on the road as 6-point road favorites with a healthy implied team total of 28.5 points (fourth). There are truly no excuses here.

Jayden Daniels has been the QB15, QB3, QB7 and QB16 in his healthiest games. Since returning from an elbow dislocation, the Commanders have yet to call a designed run for him. He'll still scramble like a madman, but more of those are resulting in slides and running out of bounds early, too. This will hurt him on the margins, though he's still the QB1 in rushing yards per game (47.2).

The Rams present a fun matchup dynamic, ranking 21st in half PPR points allowed to opposing QBs (17.0 per game) but fourth in points allowed specifically to QB rushing. Opposing offenses facing Los Angeles also feature a 47% neutral pass rate, the third-lowest rate allowed in the NFL. Washington hosts this contest as 5.5-point home underdogs carrying a 21.0-point implied team total (23rd).

10. Trevor Lawrence (at BAL) - 18.8 proj. points

Lawrence is up to QB2 overall in EPA per dropback. He hasn't been pushed many times this year, so he's left fourth quarters without adding to his totals. He's only the QB16 in yards per game, leaving him with QB8 fantasy points per game. That's closer to his floor while everyone is healthy on offense. His ceiling is far higher when games are tighter.

That's the issue with the Thursday Night Game against third-string QB Joe Fagnano. The Ravens are 10-point dogs with a team total below 15 points. The Ravens defense is far more talented and far better coached than their current No. 15 ranking in fantasy points allowed to QBs, too. Nice field position and the occasional 1-on-1 loss downfield with Marlon Humphrey in coverage are enough to combat these minor risks for T-Law as a low-end QB1. The Jaguars are ninth in team total (24.75).

Shough is off the bye and it's possible WR Jordyn Tyson makes his debut and LT Kelvin Banks (ankle) returns after a prolonged absence. Those would be major upgrades. Shough's been the QB7 in fantasy points per game but was exposed versus the Vikings and Steelers recently while dealing with a broken non-throwing hand. He should be largely over that now, and the added rushing element has made him a low-end QB1 at times in the fastest-paced offense:

Week 9 presents a much tighter test against Cleveland, as the Browns rank 26th in half PPR points allowed to opposing QBs (15.2 per game). They haven't been tested by above-average QBs this entire month, however, with Aaron Rodgers, Cam Ward, Tyler Huntley and Geno Smith in their recent history. The Browns play man coverage more than anyone else, but the Saints have the WRs to hang in there. New Orleans is listed as a 2.5-point home favorite with a 23-point team total (14th).

Love is playing the sport like nobody else. His absurd aggressiveness and arm talent are amazing to watch every week.

Love has turned into the QB9 on a per-game basis, with the second-most yards per game (281). His top three pass catchers are healthy, and they're starting to figure out the best starting OL and ways to hide them on the margins. He's scored multiple touchdowns in 7 of 8 starts. Even with Josh Jacobs in the fold, the Packers have maintained their pass-first identity. That's how it should be behind this OL.

His Week 9 draw is tougher on paper against a New England defense that ranks 22nd in half PPR points allowed to quarterbacks (16.6). The Packers head on the road as 3-point road underdogs with an implied team total of 22.25 points (17th). That's lower than normal.

The Dolphins are definitely better in November than in September. This is the youngest team in the NFL, and it's a new scheme with a new QB. The team is gelling, while also getting healthier at OL and WR. Willis has been fantasy's QB7 since Week 5 on QB14 EPA per dropback. Some of that is because of a Caleb Douglas sideline YAC TD, but him being in the lineup is helpful! Miami is still 30th in neutral pass rate (44%), but they'll be forced to pass here.

The Lions have allowed the most fantasy points to QBs, and opposing offenses have the third-highest neutral pass rate allowed. Their run defense is simply better than their secondary. Detroit will miss star LB Jack Campbell over the middle (and I really hope things progress for him, beyond football), but they did get star S Brian Branch back recently. The Lions have given up the seventh-most rushing QB points this year, partially because of garbage time scrambles. Those count for us! Willis will try to continue this top-12 QB streak, after Kyler Murray (QB10), Jordan Love (QB12), Jacoby Brissett (QB7) and Bryce Young (QB7) have done just that this month. Miami carries a 21.75-point implied team total (18th) as 7-point home underdogs.

Stafford doesn't look fully healthy right now. Some of his sideline and downfield attempts lack his usual zip, and his throwing motion is feeling a little more Philip Rivers-like. I wasn't surprised to see Monday's report that his elbow is sore. That's what his throwing motion looks like right now, and the offense being extremely quick-hitting is further evidence of just that. Over the past month, Stafford's average depth of target is at just 5.4 yards downfield. The good news is the Rams have remained top-five in neutral pass rate in the two games without Davante Adams. Stafford is a lower-ceiling QB2 right now. He is fantasy's QB16 this year on QB16 EPA per dropback.

He now faces a Washington defense allowing the third-most half PPR points per game to quarterbacks (22.0). Opposing passers have found plenty of success against the Commanders, who rank sixth in QB passing points allowed per game. Jameis Winston tagged them for 326 yards and three passing scores in Week 5, while Brock Purdy posted 17.4 fantasy points the following week with backups at WR. The Rams enter as 5.5-point road favorites with a 26.5-point implied team total (eighth).

1. Brock Bowers (at SF) - 76.5 proj. yards

Bowers' 17.2 half PPR points per game are on flirting with the best of all time at the position. He's barely even a tight end in this Klint Kubiak offense, one that's experimenting at RB and OG because the ground game is so broken. He peaked just last week with a season-high 24.7 half PPR points on 21.0 expected points. That rarely happens. Same with his 40% target share.

I know the 49ers have LB Fred Warner roaming the middle, but the 10-point spread is going to lead to a hilarious amount of targets on the road here. This is a good matchup on game script alone.

2. George Kittle (vs. LV) - 68.5 proj. yards

We'll see if Christian McCaffrey (hamstring) or Mike Evans (Achilles) will play later in the week, but it doesn't sound like Deebo Samuel Sr. will be back. This past month with injuries at WR, Kittle has been the TE4 on TE5 usage, commanding a 29% share in each of the past two games with Evans in and out of the lineup.

San Francisco draws a Raiders defense giving up 14.8 half PPR points per game to tight ends, the fourth-most in the league. The 49ers are 9.5-point home favorites with an implied team total of 31.25 points (first). Smash spot, duh.

3. Trey McBride (at SEA) - 59.5 proj. yards

The Cardinals no longer have the following skill players: Marvin Harrison Jr., Kendrick Bourne, Jalen Brooks, Reggie Virgil, Jeremiyah Love and Bam Knight. McBride was already the TE1 overall in usage, but his rare volume is further insulated. Gardner Minshew is known for his garbage-time dropbacks.

Of course, it's never a fun time against the Seahawks defense. They've allowed the fewest passing yards per game (169.1) and the fourth-fewest points to TEs. Colston Loveland (65 yards), Travis Kelce (44) and George Kittle (24) all had fairly quiet outings against them this month. McBride has battled against them recently, too. He was the TE9 on TE2 usage in Week 2, while finishing as the TE1, TE15, TE7 and TE2 in his other games against Mike Macdonald. A 14.75-point team total is scary for his touchdown odds, even if he'll likely see 8+ targets again.

4. Colston Loveland (vs. TB) - 50.5 proj. yards

Since Week 6, when Caleb Williams returned, Loveland is the TE3 on TE4 usage, largely competing with George Kittle for that No. 3 spot behind the true elites of Brock Bowers and Trey McBride.

5. Sam LaPorta (at MIA) - 43.5 proj. yards

6. Tyler Warren (vs. DAL) - 41.5 proj. yards

7. Isaiah Likely (at PHI) - 45.5 proj. yards

#2931. Elon Musk's Tesla and SpaceX lose over $420 billion in combined market cap after midterms

SpaceX and Tesla shares declined by about 9% and 11%, respectively, on Thursday afternoon with investors concerned that Elon Musk could face new congressional probes — and his companies stricter regulation — following the U.S. midterm elections.

The declines, which correlated with a broader tech sell-off, brought aggregate market cap losses for SpaceX and Tesla to over $420 billion since Tuesday's close.

Musk's wealth, which had topped $1 trillion after SpaceX's initial public offering in June, is now down below $800 billion, having dropped about $90 billion in the past week, according to Forbes. He is still the richest man in the world.

Musk was the biggest donor to GOP candidates and causes in the midterms, with his financial contributions exceeding $470 million, the Financial Times first reported.

Despite his bolstering the GOP war chest, most of the candidates Musk backed lost their races.

Democrats won control of both the House and Senate. And in Texas, where both Tesla and SpaceX are headquartered, Democrats Sen.-elect James Talarico and Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa beat longtime Musk allies, state Attorney General Ken Paxton and Gov. Greg Abbott.

Deutsche Bank analysts wrote in a note out on Wednesday, "While policy is naturally hard to predict, we could envision SpaceX facing some incremental headwinds in this type of backdrop."

Gov.-elect Hinojosa "made data centers a major campaign issue," the analysts noted, calling for "a moratorium on new approvals until the legislature passes stronger protections on water use, electricity costs, local control, and incentives," and criticizing tax breaks for large facilities.

Tesla and SpaceX are both building out AI infrastructure in Texas, and are planning to build Terafab, a massive facility, which will include a data center and sprawling semiconductor factory, in Grimes County, Texas.

SpaceX also plans to build out a "supercomputer" at its Starbase, Texas, launch facility and company town, according to company job listings.

Deutsche Bank analysts also wrote that SpaceX's Texas compute capacity could now be at risk of delays. Capital expenditures by the company may need to increase to meet the requirements of tighter, anticipated regulations around data center buildout, the analysts wrote.

Musk and SpaceX have been instructed to preserve records concerning restrictions imposed on a military version of the Starlink satellite internet service in Ukraine, CNN first reported, citing an aide to a Democratic senator. The restrictions hampered the country's ability to defend against attacks by Russia.

Additionally, regulatory scrutiny could block chances for Musk to merge SpaceX and Tesla, an idea he has promoted, while acknowledging shareholders' approval would be required first.

SpaceX and Tesla had lost about $456 billion in market cap by the close of business on Thursday.

--CNBC's Nick Wells contributed reporting.

#2930. The October jobs report is out Friday. Here's everything to expect

The U.S. economy isn't expected to have produced much job growth in October, with employers reluctant to hire workers but also not cutting staff amid a shrinking labor force and a growing problem of long-term unemployment.

Economists are looking for a meager nonfarm payrolls gain of 59,000, a slight uptick from the 29,000 jobs produced in September, according to the Dow Jones consensus. That result would continue a pattern of sparse growth.

But that's kind of OK: The forecast also is that the unemployment rate will hold at a very low 4.2% rate, the product of a climate where a stagnant labor force reduces the need for outsized job increases to keep the jobless level steady.

"Long-term, we're not going to need as many jobs added to the economy to have stable employment because we have fewer people who are available to work," said Nicole Bachaud, labor economist at jobs site ZipRecruiter. "An aging workforce is pushing a lot of people into retirement. We have had decades of low birth rates, meaning we don't have a lot of people who are coming in to replace those retiring workers. And immigration is now down pretty significantly, which is reducing some of that prime-age employment."

Evaluating the health of the labor market in such a climate becomes a moving target, Bachaud said.

More clues will come Friday when the Bureau of Labor Statistics releases the October nonfarm payrolls report at 8:30 a.m. ET.

"Do we have an economy where we have a lot of productivity but not as many jobs? Do we start focusing on things like wage growth? Is that more of a signal, especially for lower-wage workers?" she said. "I think that's the situation we're in right now because we're not going to go back to 200,000-plus jobs being added a month. That's just not the the scenario that that we're sitting in."

Indeed, payroll growth from January 2025 through September 2026 averaged just 35,000. There has been just one month during that period when gains topped 200,000 and seven months have seen losses.

Yet the unemployment rate has barely budged, up just 0.2 percentage point despite the marked slowdown.

During the period, though, the labor force has contracted by 434,000 and is down by just shy of a million since September 2025. The share of the labor force either working or looking for employment, known as the labor force participation rate, also has pulled back sharply, down to 61.8%, or a 0.8 percentage point slide.

But while hiring has been weak, other aspects show strength.

The level of layoffs has been meager, with initial jobless claims sliding to 189,000 last week, the lowest since July and hovering around the lowest level since 1969. Job openings also picked up by 333,000 in September, while the level of workers quitting their jobs — a proxy for confidence in being able to find new work — hit 3.36 million, its highest level in a year and a half.

Conversely, long-term unemployment has been a problem. The total number of workers out of a job for more than 25 weeks is up by 495,000 since January 2025, while the average duration of unemployment is at 24.8 weeks, an increase of 2.8 weeks, according to BLS data through September.

Federal Reserve officials generally consider the labor market as solid, but questions remain.

"If you have a job, you're fine, right?" ZipRecruiter's Bachaud said. "There's not a lot of movement happening, but at least you're employed. For people who are out of work because of the slowdown in turnover, people aren't vacating their jobs, employers aren't expanding and adding new jobs. That makes it very difficult to kind of break back in."

#2926. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2919. Renewed U.S.-Iran tension could push oil past $100: Rapidan Energy

The U.S. is likely to resume military action against Iran in the coming days, a move that could send oil prices sharply higher, according to Bob McNally, CEO of geopolitical and energy analysis firm Rapidan Energy Group.

Speaking to CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia," McNally put the odds of "a resumption of military fighting" at 80% as the U.S. midterm elections recede.

McNally said he expects both Brent and West Texas Intermediate crude futures to rise above $100 a barrel. The benchmarks are currently trading at about $100 and $91, respectively.

Oil prices had spiked above the $100 mark ahead of the midterm elections as investors weighed uncertainty surrounding the Iran conflict and the risk of further U.S. military actions.

President Donald Trump had previously said he would not launch new attacks on Iran before the elections, though The Wall Street Journal revealed that Washington had carried out limited strikes, according to U.S. officials.

"President Donald Trump is determined, as a legacy item, almost at all costs, to prevent Iran from getting a nuclear weapon, having them give up their nuclear capabilities," McNally said.

"The Iranian regime, on the other hand, is not willing to do it. And as long as that core difference in that deal space is absent, the escalation dynamic remains in place," he added.

McNally's view is shared by other analysts, including Lombard Odier's chief economist Samy Chaar and head of investment strategy Luca Bindelli.

In a Nov.4 note, they said the "lame duck" period before the new Congress is sworn in could give Trump a window to resume strikes against Iran. Any renewed campaign, however, could be constrained in duration and effectiveness by pressure on U.S. munitions supplies.

Other analysts see the midterm results as creating more room for diplomacy.

Norbert Rücker, head of economics and next generation research at Julius Baer said the market had become "more convinced" that the end of the midterms could provide a diplomatic off-ramp rather than lead to renewed escalation.

That possibility increased with Iran reportedly sending the U.S. a nuclear proposal through Qatar, Reuters reported Friday, citing a senior Iranian official.

But the proposal would be discussed only after Washington meets what Tehran described as "seven demands," ‌including an end to U.S. "acts of aggression," the naval blockade of Iranian ports and what it called economic warfare, as well as the release of Iranian assets.

In return, Iran will allow ships to proceed freely through the Strait of Hormuz.

The official also told Reuters that Washington was reviewing the proposal but ​declined to provide further details.

Separately, Iran's speaker of parliament Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf took aim at U.S. Vice President JD Vance after Vance blamed the war with Iran for Republicans' midterm losses.

In a post on X, Ghalibaf described Vance's reaction as "losing gambler energy" and said that Vance "can't walk away from the table." He also accused the Trump administration of preparing the next phase of conflict.

"Skip the TED talk. Why not say this to your boss?" Ghalibaf wrote, in an apparent reference to Trump.

Vance posted on Nov. 4 that "the reason we lost is the war. The president himself said so last night," adding that a pollster he consulted found that ending the war was the top issue cited by former Trump voters who had switched to Democrats. Cost of living and inflation were also among these concerns.

Vance has at times pushed for diplomacy with Tehran. The Wall Street Journal reported last week that he had reached out to an Iranian hard-liner in an effort to revive peace talks.

The war has also contributed to higher global energy costs, which have fed into broader concerns over inflation and the cost of living.

#2914. Alex Highsmith reveals what has helped the Steelers’ defense play at a high level

The Pittsburgh Steelers defense held the Cleveland Browns to season lows in points and rushing yards in their dominant 27-6 victory at Acrisure Stadium this past Sunday. As they enter the bye, the Steelers defense ranks third in scoring defense, allowing just 18 points per game through eight games. However, it wasn't always sunshine and rainbows for the defensive unit early in the season.

In fact, the first time the Steelers and Browns met this season, T.J. Watt was caught yelling into a headset on the sideline at defense coordinator Patrick Graham after a miscommunication led to a Cleveland touchdown. After that game, Graham moved from the booth to the sidelines with his players. The Steelers have rattled off four consecutive wins since Graham moved to the sidelines, backed by exceptional defensive play.

Appearing on "NFL Report" with Andrew Siciliano, Steelers edge rusher Alex Highsmith detailed how Graham's move down to the sidelines has changed the Steelers' defense.

"You know, I do believe that was a turning point for us," Highsmith said. "Obviously, he's been on the field with us ever since and so just him being down there is just really helped us a lot, for sure. But, you know PG's been awesome, ever since he came in he's just had a desire for us to be great. He's a football guy, he knows football so well, and so I just think as we continue to gel as a unit, gel as a defense, you know it's only going to be better from here on out. Like I said, I just love the way we are progressing as a defense so we just gotta continue to play like we have been and with him (Graham) calling plays, just excited to see what he continues to scheme up to put guys in positions to make plays."

Pittsburgh boasts the third most takeaways in the league with 13, while allowing just 83.9 rushing yards per game to opponents at the midway point in their season. It seems like Pittsburgh finally has the right man leading the charge defensively in Graham. Highsmith's praise of the scheme and putting guys in positions to make plays is a great sign that the players have bought into what Graham is selling them. The Steelers look well on their way to having their first season with a top-ten scoring defense since 2023, and it seems like it only took a simple move to the sidelines to make a world of difference.

#2912. Nasdaq sell-off batters Asian chip stocks as SK Hynix, Samsung, Softbank, TSMC tumble

Asian technology stocks tumbled Friday, tracking a sharp sell-off on Wall Street overnight that sent the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite into correction territory as renewed concerns over artificial intelligence spending rattled investor sentiment.

South Korean chipmaker SK Hynix plunged over 8%, while Samsung Electronics shed 7%. LG Innotek and LG Display fell 10% and 7.7%, respectively, as selling pressure spread across the country's technology sector.

The rout came as investors worried that Democrats' sweeping victory in the U.S. midterm elections could lead to tighter restrictions on data-center development, potentially slowing demand for AI chips and related infrastructure.

In Japan, semiconductor-related stocks also fell, with chip-testing equipment maker Advantest plunging 12.2%, while Tokyo Electron slid 7.3%. Technology investment giant SoftBank Group tumbled 11.6%.

Shares of memory chipmaker Kioxia Holdings fell nearly 10%, while Renesas Electronics declined 7.5%.

In Taiwan, semiconductor stocks also came under heavy selling pressure, with contract chipmaker TSMC sliding 9.6%. Electronics manufacturers Hon Hai Precision Industry, also known as Foxconn, and Foxconn Technology fell 9.4% and 9.1%, respectively, as the regional technology rout deepened.

China was also caught in the regional sell-off, with the tech-heavy STAR Market and the ChiNext Artificial Intelligence Index both tumbling more than 4%.

The declines followed a brutal session on Wall Street, where the Nasdaq Composite sank 5.5%, amid mounting concerns over the sustainability of the AI investment boom and returns on massive data-center spending. It closed more than 10% below its recent peak. The Philadelphia Semiconductor Index plunged 12.2%.

The sell-off in Asian equities has been exacerbated by investors unwinding positions, stop-loss selling and heightened volatility, Timothy Moe, chief Asia Pacific equity strategist at Goldman Sachs, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Friday.He added that potential political opposition to U.S. data center construction may affect demand and profits for Asian AI hardware suppliers.

"If there's concern about the speed and magnitude of buildout in the U.S., then obviously that's going to put all that into question," he said. "The market's tendency is always to shoot first and ask questions later. I think that's exactly what's happening now."
