#2911. Development was the plan. So why is Maluach watching from the bench?

After yet another double-digit loss on Wednesday night, this time to the Portland Trail Blazers (again), the Phoenix Suns find themselves at 1-5. They are a team desperately searching for an identity. Players are trying to establish defined roles, while the coaching staff continues experimenting with combinations in hopes of finding something that can ultimately translate into success.

When you take a step back and look at the bigger picture as it pertains to the Suns' future, things begin to look rather grim. Between the significant amount of dead cap money on the books and the fact that Phoenix has surrendered control of its draft assets for the next several seasons, there was one thing we understood entering this year that could help keep the franchise competitive: development.

After all, continuity and development were the two pillars this organization leaned on throughout the summer. The belief was that returning so many familiar faces would provide a foundation for improvement, while the younger players would take meaningful steps forward in their progression. That was the formula. That was the plan. Yet through six games, the continuity has been noticeably absent, and the opportunities for development have been puzzling.

Yes, the organization was forced to adjust its developmental timeline when Mark Williams suffered a shoulder injury in September that required surgery on his left shoulder. Suddenly, the buffer that would have allowed Khaman Maluach to develop behind Williams and Oso Ighodaro in the rotation was gone. The second-year big man out of Duke was now positioned to receive considerably more playing time than the organization likely anticipated entering the season.

While that certainly wasn't the original plan, there was a potential silver lining. Reluctance to play Maluach could no longer serve as an excuse for limiting his opportunities. The Suns were going to need him out of sheer necessity, and that necessity could provide the very developmental experience this organization had spent the summer emphasizing.

Those opportunities have certainly presented themselves early on this season. Through the first six games of the season, Khaman Maluach has started all six, averaging 7.2 points, 6.0 rebounds, 1.2 assists, 0.5 steals, and 1.0 blocks in 19.4 minutes per game.

The opportunity to develop has been there, but I've noticed a troubling trend recently that has me asking one simple question: What are we doing here? Because winning basketball games certainly isn't what we're doing right now, and of the many reasons why that's the case, the minutes being given to Khaman Maluach aren't anywhere near my top 10.

In fact, his absence becomes increasingly noticeable on a team that desperately lacks size and has been horrific at consistently securing rebounds. Phoenix ranks 29th in the NBA in rebounds per game at 39.5 and dead last in rebound percentage at 45.3%. Yet despite those struggles, the Suns continue to limit the playing time of one of the few players on the roster capable of providing a legitimate interior presence.

And that brings me to the trend that's becoming increasingly difficult to understand. In each of the last three games, Khaman Maluach hasn't played a single minute in the fourth quarter. Against both Denver and Golden State, he was pulled with 7:04 remaining in the third. Against Portland on Wednesday night, his final appearance ended with 6:09 left in the third. Why?

Last night against the Trail Blazers, Jordan Ott followed the same substitution pattern we've grown accustomed to seeing early this season. Maluach starts the game before exiting midway through the first quarter. He returns midway through the second, plays roughly five minutes, and then starts the second half. But somewhere around the midway point of the third quarter, Ott pulls him from the game, never to be seen again.

We've now witnessed that exact pattern in four of the Suns' first six games, and for a team supposedly committed to development, it's becoming increasingly difficult to understand why. So let's try to figure it out.

Khaman Maluach Substitution Patterns | https://nbarotations.info/player/1642863

Without Khaman Maluach, the Suns are left relying on either small-ball lineups or Oso Ighodaro at the five. And while neither option is inherently problematic, the results have been inconsistent, particularly on the boards.

Last night against Portland, Phoenix was actually holding its own on the glass. Yes, the Suns were trailing by 13 points when Maluach exited with 6:09 remaining in the third quarter, but they were winning the rebounding battle 27-25. Over the final 18:09, however, Portland outrebounded Phoenix by 10 and won the offensive rebounding battle 10-5. By the final buzzer, the Trail Blazers had secured a 47-39 advantage on the glass.

It's important to note that these numbers reflect the team's performance before and after Maluach's final substitution, rather than exclusively when he was on or off the court.

The data tells a slightly different story against Golden State. Through the first 28:56, Phoenix had been outrebounded 28-20. But over the final 19:04, after Maluach exited for the final time, the Suns actually outrebounded the Warriors 17-16. Against Denver, Phoenix held a 22-19 rebounding advantage before Maluach's final substitution, then was outrebounded 21-20 the rest of the way.

So what gives? Why is Maluach being banned from the chat after the two-and-a-half quarters of play? Across these three games, Phoenix was outrebounded 72-69 before Maluach's final departures and 59-49 afterward. That's a three-rebound deficit becoming a 10-rebound deficit, despite the latter periods being considerably shorter.

Welp, we're done here. It's clear Ott doesn't know what he's doing. But wait. As Rafiki once wisely said, "Look harder…"

Rather than evaluating the rebounding numbers based on the point at which Maluach permanently exits the game, let's examine how Phoenix actually performs on the glass when he's on the court compared with when he's sitting on the bench.

Against Portland, Maluach played 15:51, during which the Suns were outrebounded 16-12, with the Trail Blazers collecting three offensive rebounds. During the 32:09 he spent on the bench, Portland still held the advantage, though the margin was considerably smaller, 31-27. Phoenix secured 42.9% of the recorded rebounds with Maluach playing, compared with 46.6% when he was off the floor.

Against Golden State, the disparity was even more noticeable. Phoenix was down seven rebounds in the rebounding battle with Maluach on the court and even when he was on the bench. The Suns secured only 36.0% of available rebounds during his minutes, compared with 50.0% when he wasn't playing.

And against Denver? Phoenix was even on the glass with Maluach playing and +2 with him on the bench. The Suns collected 50.0% of recorded rebounds when he was on the court and 51.7% when he was off.

Looking at the three games in which Malauch was pulled early, cumulatively, you find that when Malauch was on the court for 46:23, the team was outbounded by 11. Off the court for 97:37? The team was -2.

There's one variable in all of this that we simply don't have an answer to. We don't know how Maluach would perform in these fourth quarters if he were actually given the opportunity. We can evaluate what happens during his minutes and compare those numbers with what occurs when he's on the bench.

The numbers certainly complicate the argument that Maluach's absence is directly responsible for Phoenix's rebounding struggles. In these three games, the Suns actually rebounded at a higher percentage when he wasn't on the floor, although the degree of that advantage varied considerably from game to game. That's an interesting finding, especially given the eye test against Portland, where the Suns appeared to struggle considerably after his final departure. The full on/off data paints a more nuanced picture.

Khaman Maluach: Season On/Off | https://hoopsjunkie.io/player/khaman-maluach

This is where the conversation becomes particularly interesting, because at what point do you prioritize development over rebounding percentages when a player is on or off the court? Maluach hasn't looked great to start the season, but he's certainly been serviceable. And for a team that desperately lacks size, he's somebody who should probably be seeing a little more playing time at this point.

But in the grand scheme of things, when you're looking at a three-game sample in which the rebounding differential is minus-nine, I don't believe that's enough to justify limiting the opportunities and potential growth of a young center who needs experience to develop. Especially when you consider that Maluach has consistently demonstrated a desire to compete and remain engaged. This isn't somebody whose struggles stem from a poor attitude or a lack of effort.

He's a young player learning how to navigate the NBA, and perhaps we're overanalyzing a handful of fleeting moments rather than allowing him to work through those experiences.

You have to eventually take the training wheels off and let the kid learn, right? Is it too soon? Apparently, the coaching staff believes so.

But at some point, the Suns have to decide what they're actually trying to accomplish this season. If winning is the priority, the results suggest their current approach isn't working. And if development is supposed to be the foundation for the future, Maluach needs opportunities to make mistakes, learn from them, and grow. Those objectives don't have to be mutually exclusive. But keeping the training wheels on while the team continues losing accomplishes very little. Especially when the kid is never given a chance to finish the ride.

#2908. Oil prices rise as investors weigh renewed U.S.-Iran conflict risks after midterm elections

Oil prices rose Friday as investors weighed the risk of renewed military escalation between the U.S. and Iran following the midterm elections, alongside prospects for a diplomatic breakthrough.

Brent crude futures climbed 1.67% to $99.90 per barrel, while U.S. West Texas Intermediate crude futures gained 1.43% to $91.69 per barrel.

The gains came as investors assessed whether the conclusion of the U.S. midterm elections could pave the way for renewed actions against Iran, potentially threatening oil supplies from the Middle East.

Bob McNally, Rapidan Energy Group founder and president, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" that he sees an 80% probability of military fighting resuming, forecasting that both oil benchmarks could climb above $100 a barrel.

However, hopes for a diplomatic resolution have also emerged after Iran reportedly sent a nuclear proposal to Washington through Qatar, Reuters reported, citing a senior Iranian official.

Iran reported three additional attacks on oil tankers in the Strait of Hormuz on Thursday, while data from the UK Maritime Trade Operations (UKMTO) indicated that attacks on vessels were continuing at a near-record rate.

"President Donald Trump is determined, as a legacy item, almost at all costs, to prevent Iran from getting a nuclear weapon, having them give up their nuclear capabilities," McNally said.

"The Iranian regime, on the other hand, is not willing to do it. And as long as that core difference in that deal space is absent, the escalation dynamic remains in place," he added.

McNally's view is shared by other analysts, including Lombard Odier's chief economist Samy Chaar and head of investment strategy Luca Bindelli.

—CNBC's Lim Hui Jie contributed to this report.

#2907. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2898. Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

#2895. LeBron James' lawyers send cease and desist to AI sports-betting app that used his name and likeness

Attorneys for Philadelphia 76ers star LeBron James sent a cease and desist letter to an AI sports-betting app that used a popular nickname for James and released an ad featuring his likeness, according to Front Office Sports.

Bron AI, a sports-gambling app, put out the ad Wednesday. In it, an AI version of James can be seen placing bets on NBA outcomes.

The ad, which has already been taken down on X, never showed James' face in AI, but showed a basketball player who looked a lot like James from behind. The player in the ad was wearing a No. 23 jersey that read "Bron" on the back, making it an exceptionally obvious reference to James.

In the cease and desist letter, which was obtained by Front Office Sports, James' attorneys make it clear James "neither approved any use and/or exploitation of, nor authorized or granted permission" to his name or likeness being used in the ad.

Additionally, James' attorneys pointed out that the 76ers star already has an "exclusive endorsement arrangement" with Polymarket and that the Bron AI ad appeared to show James' character placing bets on NBA games, which is "a very serious violation of NBA rules and regulations."

James is, apparently, not the only NBA star to issue a cease and desist letter to the company. James' former teammate, Los Angeles Lakers star Luka Dončić, reportedly also asked the company to stop using his likeness. Dončić's picture was used in the ad. #2894. CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."

#2892. Broncos pass rush has returned with a fury through eight games

While the sack numbers are a bit off pace to the previous two seasons, the pressure rate is actually higher. The Denver Broncos defense is forcing quarterbacks to get rid of the ball quick or face the consequences.

144 quarterback pressures. 12 more than second place.

74 combined sacks and QB hits. 15 more than second place.

51 quarterback hits. 11 more than second place.

PFF's top ranked pass rush grade. Best pass rush win rate.

The reason the sacks are off pace is due to the competition this defense has faced. These quarterbacks are really good at avoiding sacks: Patrick Mahomes x2, Trevor Lawrence, Matthew Stafford, and Brock Purdy. Those guys have a combined 41 sacks between them. The other three quarterbacks Denver faced: Justin Herbert, Jacoby Brisset, and Sam Darnold have a combined 59 sacks between them.

Of the remaining eight quarterbacks on the schedule, four quarterbacks are in the Top 5 in sacks taken and all but Josh Allen and Kirk Cousins are in the Top 10. Those sack numbers should start to stack for this Broncos defense down the stretch.

Broncos focused on taking care of business against Panthers

The Denver Broncos have a shot to be 7-2 before their bye week if they can topple the Carolina Panthers on Sunday.

How the Denver Broncos can beat the Carolina Panthers

The Denver Broncos head on the road to face the Carolina Panthers in Week 9. Here is how they can earn their seventh win of the season.

Broncos vs. Panthers: 3 Things to Watch in Week 9

Here are three storylines to watch when the Denver Broncos battle the Carolina Panthers on Sunday.

Alex Singleton named AFC Defensive Player of the Month

What a turnaround from Week 1 for Alex Singleton and the entire defense. Singleton especially has been balling out during the Denver Broncos four game win streak!

On the defense's performance in goal-line situations

"I think we just play. We don't worry about where the ball's put down. We just play. Those things we practice every single week. I'm not surprised that we're good down there. We spent a bunch of time down there. It's been our fourth year together. We have our four or five calls we love down there that we're good at. So if we do it right, we expect to win."

On if he's surprised that ILB Alex Singleton wasn't previously recognized for his high level of play

"No, that's the league. When you're playing well on defense, guys do get recognized. He's been consistent for a long time. He had 175 tackles [three] years ago. No one talks about that. I'm not surprised he's playing well again."

On young players like DE Sai'vion Jones and OLB Que Robinson being ready to play

"It's coaching. I think when you're picking your roster, you have to pick your roster with guys that fit your scheme. I think here that's what we've done. So when you lose one player, we have guys who can fill in who have the same skill set. So I'm not surprised those kids play well. Again it's NFL football. It's the same coach, it's the same room. When it's their turn to play, they play to the standard. If they don't fit and they can't play, they shouldn't be here. Every guy on this team who makes our roster on defense, if they have to play for us, nothing changes in my mind. Let's go win the game. There are certain areas where I can't call certain things with certain players, but the standard of doing it right, that never changes here."

On determining special teams rotations during games

"I think one of the things people might not recognize is we do a pretty good job of keeping track of reps during the game. We have guys up in the box kind of doing a rep count and making sure no one's getting too many. As you know, there are some games where you might have 18 special teams plays, and there are some games where you might have 35. It really just depends how the game plays out. So we'll do a good job. We'll have a plan kind of going in. Dispel those guys if needed. They're going to have to do obviously some things. They might not do the full workload as they normally do. Then again, they might depending on how the game plays out. So we'll kind of track that as we go. Yes, my depth chart sheet will be an interesting one. Be like the old, what's the movie? The old Matrix. It'll be a lot of stuff going on there."

On the challenges of kicking field goals and extra points in the rain and how they prepare for those conditions

"I think obviously because we play outdoors at our home games, weekly we'll maybe do some wet ball drills and things like that. It's weird when the K-balls are a different ball than the quarterback ball. They're a little bit smoother because they get kind of worked. So when those balls get a little bit wet, they actually get really slippery. It's not the ball that's soaked, it's the ball when it just starts raining. If you've talked to all the specialists over the years, the snappers and the holders and the punter catching the operation, those are the ones. So right away when you see any precipitation come down, those guys are off on the side and they're getting work and whether or not they use a towel, gloves, whatever we're allowed to use. Yes, it certainly makes that operation. So as soon as we know we're going to get any kind of weather, we certainly practice it. Then those guys on gameday are going to be working on the sideline and gauging the weather."

On the challenges of kicking at sea level when they're accustomed to altitude

"I think the pregame warmup for us may be more important than any other position because you kind of get an idea of how the ball's going to travel on that particular day, whether it's wind, weather. I've coached at sea level for more than 16 years in the NFL and then a bunch of years in college. The ball may not travel as well. We'll see what the weather is there. The pregame for the kicker and the punter is probably more important than people would anticipate because you kind of get the wind direction, you know which way if it's sideways wind or north-south wind, if you will. Then how well the ball's traveling. So we kind of have, [Head] Coach [Sean Payton] will always ask me right before kickoff, 'What's our line going each way for us and them?' So the ball certainly travels a little bit better here in altitude, but I think sometimes it gets overblown, overdid a little bit. We'll see how it is there in pregame on Sunday morning."

On the spark RB Jonah Coleman brings to the offense

"I think that started since rookie minicamp, and then the preseason game against Atlanta, and then the Jacksonville game and now this game. I think every time he gets an opportunity, he's showed up. His teammates love him. He's smart, he's mature and he's going to continue to get better, which is awesome."

On how Head Coach Sean Payton has prepared the team for its next game following an emotional win

"I think if you look at Carolina's record, you can be one of those guys that overlooks them. If you look at the Chicago game, they put up a lot of points on offense. You look at the Detroit game, they beat them. There are plenty of examples of this team being really, really good there. A playoff team last year, arguably should have beat the Rams in the playoffs last year. So they're a couple plays away from having a winning record. This is the NFL. There are not many cupcakes out there, so this is going to be a good one on Sunday."

On the challenge of facing a team with a new defensive coordinator

"I think the first quarter's going to be interesting in regards to seeing what they have new, what's kind of the same. [They have] a couple of players that are kind of banged up. Are they going to play or are they not? Obviously schematics. So I think the first quarter's going to be a really interesting quarter. Then I think the game's going to settle down within that second to the fourth. You could say the same thing about Kansas City versus us. Obviously it's still [Head Coach] Sean [Payton]'s offense. There wasn't much changed. Midseason changing a bunch is probably going to be tough, but there'll definitely be some wrinkles and we're prepared to attack it."

#2891. Lakers’ Walker Kessler surprised where he ranks in key statistical category

SACRAMENTO — Walker Kessler has been every bit of the rim protector the Lakers hoped he would be when they made the investment they did to acquire him from the Jazz.

Lakers center Walker Kessler (14) has once again been one of the league's best rim protectors. AP Photo/Bethany Baker

Opponents shoot significantly worse and less near the rim when the 7-foot-2 center is on the floor.

He's top seven in the league in defensive field-goal percentage near the rim.

And after blocking four shots during the Lakers' 116-103 win over the Kings on Wednesday, Kessler ranked third in blocked shots per game entering Thursday — a stat that surprised him.

Walker Kessler was surprised when he found out he didn't rank second in blocked shots per game. Getty Images

"Third? I know [Victor Wembanyama's] ahead of me," Kessler said. "Who else is ahead of me?"

Kessler added: "But it's crazy. I thought I was at least No. 2 That's crazy."

Wembanyama, the reigning Defensive Player of the Year, was an easy guess considering he leads the league at 4.5 blocked shots per game after blocking eight shots in the Spurs' win over the Mavericks on Wednesday.

The only other player averaging more blocked shots than Kessler? Bucks big man Myles Turner, who entered Thursday averaging 2.8 blocked shots per game compared with Kessler's 2.7.

Walker Kessler has blocked the second-most shots so far this season. AP Photo/Mark J. Terrill

"Really?" Kessler responded when this information was relayed to him.

Technically, Kessler has blocked more shots this season. He's recorded 24 blocked shots, while Turner has 22.

But Kessler's nine games played compared with Turner's eight gives the Bucks center the edge when it comes to the season-long average.

It's unlikely either will threaten Wembanyama when it comes to the most blocked shots per game this season. The Spurs 7-4 phenom has led the NBA in the statistical category each season since being the top draft pick in 2023. But the race for No. 2 will be a fun one to monitor.

#2888. Jonathan Taylor: No one likes uncertainty

Colts running back Jonathan Taylor has spent the last two weeks fielding questions about whether he’ll remain with the team and that will come to an end by next Tuesday one way or another.

Taylor will either remain with the Colts through Tuesday’s 4 p.m. ET trade deadline or he’ll be dealt to a team that thinks he can help push them over the top this season. Until he’s dealt or the deadline passes, Taylor knows “you’re not going to avoid” the questions he faced one again on Thursday and he knows that he’ll be happy when they stop coming his way.

“No one wants uncertainty,” Taylor said Thursday, via Stephen Holder of ESPN.com. “Until the past couple weeks, it’s kind of been the same thing. But it won’t be until Tuesday where you’ll know exactly what’s going on, you’ll know exactly what the verdict is.”

Taylor played amid the rumors in last week’s loss to the Jaguars and the plan is for him to play against the Cowboys this week. He said he is working to keep his focus on that game because he doesn’t “want external factors creeping in and trying to alter your play,” but that will be a much easier task to accomplish once there’s certainty about where he’ll be after November 10.

#2887. Virginia Tech Hokies vs. SMU Mustangs: News, Information, and Predictions

It's a short week after a week break. The Hokies are heading to Dallas to face off against the Southern Methodist University Mustangs. The Hokies are up against another major ACC opponent, and winning the game means advancing in the ACC and potentially seeing a ranking.

Just a note from us: Well, the first week in November through the first week of December is always a challenge for coverage. The soccer teams are wrapping up with decent enough records, but their seasons aren't quite over. The Women's Basketball Team has opened their home record at 2-0, and the Men's team also graced Cassell with a W. All of that puts a real strain on coverage so bear with us as we sort out schedules, and fight fall health issues and work vs. Hokie sports balance.

The football team isn't finished, though. There are still four games left on the schedule, and then we get to plan on bowl coverage this season. Isn't that a happy event? The Hokies are bowl eligible just a couple of games after the halfway point, and that's a very pleasant occurrence. To think that we are actually paying attention to the bowl invitation list, instead of worrying on Thanksgiving weekend whether or not a win will net an invitation somewhere.

The first thing to cover is a bit of fun. The ACC Network desk hosts Eric McClain and Eddie Royal had a bet going. Remember, McClain is a former Clemson player, and we all know and love Eddie Royal. So, the bet was that the loser had to wear the winner's mascot costume. Eddie was not dressed as a Tiger with the googly eyes for the TV segment. (Congratulations to the Virginia Tech Cheer Squad equipment folks and Travis Wells for getting most of an actual Hokie Bird big enough for Eric.)

The Virginia Tech Wrestling Team received a massive boost from a wrestler who hasn't even wrestled his first match as a Hokie, yet, but who is already a world champion. Freshman Bo Bassett traveled to Kazakhstan and netted the program its first world championship and was the first American in 20 years to win in his weight class (65KG – for those lacking metric conversion skills that's 143 pounds).

The Coach Robie is going to have an absolutely dominant wrestling team this season.

It looks like the team is going to be wearing head to toe all-whites for this one.

Go get your coffee, or a quick snack and listen in on Coach Franklin's weekly presser.

As Bryan posted up earlier, this time the Hokies have some really good news coming out of the locker room as it pertains to the Offensive Line. Montavious Cunningham and Brody Meadows are back up to speed and are expected to play on Friday night. The O-Line has been dipping into the #3s on the depth chart for the past few games, and getting them back is a serious update to the struggling run game.

The SMU Mustangs are largely a Big XII import with a near automatic bubble to the top of the ACC football pile. It's Dallas, TX, and football is a near religion for Texas. That means the program is well funded, with premium coaching, and players doused liberally with NIL funding.

Up until last weekend, the Mustangs were sporting a very respectable one loss record. Until they showed up in the Syracuse (whatever they call the Carrier Dome, now), as expected in the beginning of the season SMU was challenging hard for the #2 bid in the ACC fake Championship game (until the conference goes back to divisions and division champs who play everyone else in the division the game in Charlotte will remain fake to me). Then the Mustangs got treated to the old Big East upset win by the Orange. That knocked them down a peg but also put the Hokies in the position of earning the #3 spot on the ACC leader board.

That all means that this game might not mean an ACC bid, but it will mean a much better bowl game situation over the holidays. Bowls mean big broadcast money, and the program needs the cash to make up for a restricted gate at Lane.

The Mustangs are favored by slightly less than a touchdown for this one, in their house no less. We'll see how we feel about that.

This is a big test for the Hokies. Virginia Tech had a huge win at Clemson two weeks ago. No, this isn't the same Clemson team as only a few years ago, but it means something for other ACC teams to walk into Death Valley and win. SMU is better than the Tigers, but they aren't unbeatable. The clear difference here is the quarterback position. SMU quarterback Kevin Jennings is a stud. He also has one of the ACC's best running backs and one of the top receiving units in the league. This is a huge challenge for VT defensive coordinator Brent Pry. Offensively, I think the Hokies have success moving the ball. SMU's size on the defensive interior concerns me, especially with Tech's OL struggles. There is hope that with Cunningham and Meadows back, the offensive line is better, but we'll see.

I expect a close game. My heart says the Hokies, my head says SMU in a close one.

This game all depends on how the Mustangs adjusted to their Syracuse loss, and if the Hokies on both sides of the ball can adjust to how the Orange controlled the SMU offense, and how they managed to get by their defense. SMU, interestingly enough, has a similar offensive/defensive balance to Tech's than we have seen this season.

There are lots of "IFs" in this game, and the biggest one is "IF" the Tech defense can limit the SMU offense to what it's been scoring over the season against peer teams, the Hokies have a much better chance to win than the listed 6-point betting gimme to SMU. The latest projected O/U is 56.5, which tells me volumes of information. The analysts see this as a very close game, and the end result being less than a full score between the two teams.

If the Hokies stay away from getting enamored of the run game and go downfield fast and consistently in the air on quick developing intermediate routes to their best receivers, there will be run opportunities. If Tech decides to try to "establish the run" the offense could bog down into chronic 3-and-outs. The return of two key #1/#2 depth chart players is not an excuse to try to revert to 1960's caveman Johnny Unitas two runs and a pass offense.

If the Hokies move the ball and do it fast, they have enough defense (which needs to put serious pressure on the quarterback on every play) to hold the Mustangs to a 3.5 score effort. The Hokies are capable of 4 or more.

That's a whole lot of "Ifs" though. Here's the skinny… the first team to score over 30 points wins. That puts my prediction hat on, and I think the Hokies might just be good for it, regardless of the conditions.

#2885. Fantasy Football 2026: Week 9 preview — Start/sit and more

Welcome to Week 9. Somehow, we are already at the halfway point of the 18-week NFL season. That was fast.

Week 8 was strange. Tampa beat Atlanta 16-0 without completing a single pass. Dallas and Kansas City both blew double-digit fourth quarter leads. Joe Burrow accounted for six total touchdowns. Running backs absolutely ran wild on Sunday. Baltimore lost two quarterbacks to what might be season-ending injuries in the first half at Buffalo. Minnesota held Detroit to its lowest point total of the season, but also scored the fewest against them, and absorbed its first loss. The Giants grew their NFC East lead without playing. It was that kind of weekend – eerie and weird — kind of fitting for Halloween.

What's in store this week? Six teams – Philadelphia, Seattle, Baltimore, Minnesota, fCleveland, and Arizona – are switching to a different starting quarterback. That raises a bunch of questions and decisions for fantasy managers.

Stats of the Week, Bucs-Falcons (sorry, this HAS TO get its own section – one day you'll tell your grandkids about that time in 2026 when two NFL teams played a high school football game):

Tampa became the first team since 1974, and the third since the forward pass was invented in the 1930s, to win a game without completing a single pass.

Tampa is the first team in NFL history to have two players (Bucky Irving and Kenneth Gainwell) with 25+ rushes and 125+ rushing yards in the same game.

Tampa's defenders caught four more passes than Tampa's offensive players.

Despite not completing a pass, and rushing for just 14 yards with no TDs, Baker Mayfield had more fantasy points than, and triple the passer rating of, Michael Penix, Jr.

Over their past two games (both wins, with 59 total points scored), the Bucs called 16 passes and 104 runs, and Baker Mayfield had seven completions for 64 yards.

No team in AFC North is over .500. Every team in the NFC North is over .500. Every team in the NFC South is under .500.

The NFC North is a combined 22-3 in non-divisional games.

Bo Nix has engineered 13 fourth quarter comebacks – the most in NFL history for a quarterback in his first three seasons.

Since the start of last season, the Broncos are 3-3 in games where they trailed by 11+ points during the fourth quarter. The rest of the NFL is 11-215 in such situations.

For the first time since 2003, six running backs had at least 140 rushing yards on Sunday.

The Browns have lost 23 straight games in Pittsburgh, which ties the NFL record for a road losing streak vs. one team.

The Bears have 17 rushing touchdowns, which is the most for a team through their first eight games since 1956.

Since 2000, Arizona was 1-147-1 when trailing by 14 points or more in the fourth quarter, and Dallas was 69-0 at home when leading by 14 points or more in the fourth quarter. That all changed on Sunday with the Cardinals' late comeback.

On to Week 9. Note: CIN @ ATL is being played in Spain. Set those lineups early!

For those who are familiar with this column, you know the drill. For everyone else: The Rides, Fades, and Sleepers is an analysis of players that I think are primed for an especially strong or poor performance, often as compared to consensus expectations. This isn't a straight-up Start/Sit exercise. As a general rule, start your studs. I'll rarely list the most obvious names at a position as "Rides" because those players are almost always expected to have strong performances. You don't need me or anyone else telling you to start Joe Burrow. While this analysis is intended for season-long play, it works for DFS formats too. Half PPR scoring and Expert Consensus Rankings (ECR) are used for the column.

Each week, I'm picking a Ride, Fade, and Sleeper of the Week. The rules: The Ride of the Week can't be a truly elite option, the Fade can't be someone who nobody is starting anyway, and the Sleeper must be an actual sleeper, from down in the rankings.

Last week, my Rides, Fades, and Sleepers did well, with a lot more hits than misses. You can check my work here.

My biggest miss was my Ride of the Week, Zay Flowers. He scored zero points. I'll repeat that – A bagel. A donut. Zero point zero. I obviously couldn't foresee the quarterback injuries, but I'll take the L. My Fade of the Week hit (Bryce Young, 11.4 fantasy points, QB18) and so did my Sleeper of the Week (Geno Smith, 19 fantasy points, QB13).

Ride of the Week: Matthew Stafford (@WAS). Stafford is ranked outside the Top-10 QBs this week and I think that's too low. The Commanders can score behind Jayden Daniels and they also give up points. This game's Vegas total of 50.5 is the second highest on the board and I think it goes over. Washington is tough to run on, but generous in the passing game. They're one of seven teams that has allowed at least 15 TD passes so far this season, and they've given up the third most fantasy points per game (FPPG) to opposing QBs. Stafford is coming off his second worst fantasy output of the season and I think he'll rebound. He came out of last week's game with a sore elbow, so keep an eye on that.

Fade of the Week: Zay Flowers (vs. JAC). Well, this is a first for me, as Flowers goes from Ride of the Week to Fade of the Week in just five days. UDFA rookie Joe Fagnano gets his first NFL start for the Ravens, on a short week and a bad ankle. If the Ravens get to ten points, I'll be surprised. Their implied total is 15, which is the second lowest on the Board, and they haven't topped 13 points in any of their last three games. Jacoby Brissett will help – in Week 10. Jacksonville's defense must be licking its chops after it throttled another dead team last week. Flowers is a terrific receiver who was having a great season before last Sunday, but this QB situation is too much to overcome. He's ranked inside the Top-20 at the position this week and I have him outside my Top-30.

Sleeper of the Week: Drew Lock (vs. ARI). The Cardinals are coming off that improbable comeback in Dallas, while Seattle will look to rebound after getting spanked by the Bears at home. This should be a get-right game for the Seahawks, and a potential let-down affair for the Cardinals (who had a let-down two weeks ago, after their big upset win over the Rams in Week 6). Seattle may struggle to run the ball against a defense that just stymied Javonte Williams. Arizona has allowed the most TD passes (18) and second most FPPG to opposing QBs. Lock is ranked as the QB29 this week, which is absurd. He's behind Fagnano, Gardner Minshew II, and Shedeur Sanders? No. None of them have JSN to throw to. Obviously, he shouldn't be started over a Top-12 option, but I like him as a QB2 this week. In his only start of the season in Week 2, he put up 21 fantasy points at…you guessed it…Arizona.

Elite options this week – Josh Allen, Lamar, Drake Maye, Brock Purdy, Jared Goff, and Joe Burrow;the analysis starts below them.

Three QBs ranked in the Top-10 this week who should smash:

Dak Prescott (@IND). The Colts have given up, or at least, they don't have the ability to compete at the moment. Dak should have his way with this mediocre defense, which has allowed the tenth most FPPG to opposing QBs. Dallas can't afford another stupid loss.

Trevor Lawrence (@BAL). The Ravens have a decent defense that's middle of the pack against opposing QBs, but there's almost no scenario where they don't lose this game by a lot. Their offense won't be able to stay on the field, so Lawrence should have plenty of scoring chances.

Patrick Mahomes (vs. NYJ). Mahomes threw for 321 yards and three TDs at Denver, to break a mini-cold streak and this week he gets a much softer defense, at home. Kenneth Walker might run wild, but I think Mahomes will get his too. The Jets have allowed 14 TD passes this season and have only one pick (ahem, that's since the start of last season).

Malik Willis (vs. DET) is in play this week as a low-end QB1 streamer. He's thrown for two TDs in two straight games and added a rushing TD in Week 7. The Lions' defense is showing improvement over the last two weeks, but they're still vulnerable and while they got their safeties back, now they're without stalwart LB Jack Campbell. They've allowed the most passing yards, passing TDs (18) and FPPG to opposing QBs, and they've also yielded two rushing TDs to the position.

Other QB2-types to consider if you need it: C.J. Stroud (@LAC), Kirk Cousins (@SF), Bo Nix (@CAR), and J.J. McCarthy (@PHI). Carson Wentz (@BUF) is risky, but it's an excellent matchup.

There are some higher-end starters with less-than-ideal matchups, but none are bad enough for me to list them as Fades. However, there are some QB2s who fantasy managers could be considering this week, but that I'm avoiding if I can: Baker Mayfield (@CHI, and I'm not sure how much he can really throw right now), and Michael Penix, Jr. (vs. CIN, and if you started him last week, I'm sorry for your loss).

And don't start Justin Herbert (vs. HOU). This is now a running joke.

Elite options this week – Jahmyr Gibbs, Bijan Robinson, Kenneth Walker, and Christian McCaffrey; the analysis starts below them.

Three high-end backs in smash spots this week:

Javonte Williams (@IND) barely averaged two yards per carry last week, a week after setting a career high in rushing yards. Which Williams will we get this week? A good one, I expect. Indianapolis's run defense isn't as stout without DeForest Buckner and allows the tenth most FPPG to opposing RBs. The Colts are reeling and offered very little resistance to Jacksonville's ground game last week (the first without Buckner). Williams is among the league leaders in carries inside the five and should bounce back nicely in this spot.

Bucky Irving (@CHI) and Bhayshul Tuten (@BAL) are coming off monster games and should rack up some good totals again this week. Chicago is tougher up the gut with Jeffery Simmons in the middle, but Tampa will run it and then run it some more. The Ravens are a tough run defense, but game script is a big factor here. Jacksonville is a 10-point favorite and should dominate time of possession and play with a big lead. If Tuten is out, Chris Rodriguez, Jr. is an OK play if you need him.

Others ranked inside the Top-25 this week who I think will outperform their rankings: Chase Brown (@ATL, who now allow the third most FPPG to opposing RBs after getting run over for three straight hours on Sunday), Chuba Hubbard (vs. DEN), Jonah Coleman (@CAR, allowing the second most FPPG to opposing RBs), and Quinshon Judkins (@NO, most FPPG allowed to opposing RBs). If Chase Brown can't get the ground game going this week, I'll start to get worried.

A few to consider if you're stuck: Woody Marks (@LAC), Alvin Kamara (vs. CLE), Mike Washington, Jr. (@SF), Dylan Sampson (vs. CLE), and J.K. Dobbins (@CAR).

I've got some household names on the list this week.

Derrick Henry (vs. JAC) is a tough call this week. The Ravens are in a terrible situation for this game, on a short week. Jacksonville has been a buzzsaw for running backs. They allow the fewest yards and FPPG to opposing RBs. Henry is outside my Top-15 for the week. Henry scored last week, but his fantasy output has taken a huge hit since Lamar Jackson got hurt.

Is Jonathan Taylor (vs. DAL) really going to be active on Sunday? It's a tasty matchup (see, e.g., Allgeier, Tyler), but if he plays he could see limited snaps while potential buyers consider their offers and the Colts try to protect the asset. Start him if he's active, but with caution.

It's tough to start Saquon Barkley (@NYG), who is dealing with multiple injuries and isn't a lock to get a decent workload. Yes, he has two straight games with a TD, but in each of those games he played fewer than 20 snaps. I'm sure he still gets a little extra motivation against his old team, but they've been pretty good against running backs (Top-12 fantasy run defense).

Josh Jacobs (@NE) struggled to find running room last week and looked slow. He got bailed out with two short touchdowns, and that's his upside. Green Bay hasn't run the ball well all season, and New England allows the fifth fewest FPPG to opposing RBs. Pass.

The Seahawks struggled to run the ball consistently against Chicago, and it won't be all that much easier against Arizona, who is decent vs. the run. Throw in that Seattle is using multiple backs in rotation and I'll fade Zach Charbonnet and Jadarian Price (vs. ARI).

PSA: Don't start any Chargers RBs. Of all players who don't have injury as an excuse, Omarion Hampton is currently the single most disappointing player who went in the first two rounds of fantasy drafts.

Other RB Fades: Cam Skattebo (@PHI, if he plays), Ashton Jeanty (@SF), and Blake Corum (@WAS).

Elite options this week – Puka Nacua, Jaxon Smith-Njigba, Ja'Marr Chase AND Tee Higgins, Amon-Ra St. Brown, CeeDee Lamb, and Malik Nabers; the analysis starts below them.

I've listed seven "elites" above. Beyond them, here are six Rides this week, all ranked inside the weekly Top-20: Christian Watson AND Matthew Golden (@NE, tough matchup but they can't run the ball and will have to throw a lot), Parker Washington (to bounce back, @BAL), Rashee Rice (vs. NYJ), Justin Jefferson (vs. BUF, they've allowed the most TD catches and fifth most FPPG to opposing WRs, and the Vikings will actually need to score to win this game), and Luther Burden III (vs. TB).

Moving down to those ranked between 21 and 30 for the Week — I like these guys as WR2/WR3 or Flex plays: D.J. Moore (@MIN, tough matchup, but Moore will get a lot of 1-on-1s), Stefon Diggs (vs. LAR), Ladd McConkey (vs. HOU), and Mike Evans (vs. LV, assuming he plays).

WRs ranked outside the Top-30 this week that I think you can plug into lineups if you're in need comprise a pretty big list this week: Deebo Samuel, Sr. (vs. LV, if he plays), K.C. Concepcion, Jr. (@NO), Malik Washington AND Caleb Douglas (vs. DET), Jordan Addison (vs. BUF), Rome Odunze (vs. TB, he's making a few big catches each week and is overdue for a score), Makai Lemon (vs. NYG, and Joe Milton could be a nice upgrade for this passing game), and Ryan Flournoy (@IND).

Tet McMillan (vs. DEN) had a monster game in Week 4 but hasn't topped 12.5 fantasy points in the rest of his outings. The Broncos are a tough matchup, too (sixth fewest FPPG and just four TD catches allowed to the position).

The Jets have more weapons these days, so Garrett Wilson (@KC) is not seeing his usual massive target share. It's a tough matchup (second fewest FPPG and a league-low two TD catches allowed to opposing receivers).

I know he scored a TD and got a 2-point catch last week, but Jaylen Waddle (@CAR) is hard to trust. He played a season-low 45% of snaps last week, a troubling development. He also only had 39 receiving yards and was bailed out by the scoring plays.

I'll fade A.J. Brown (vs. GB) if he plays. He's an auto-fade to me until he looks like some semblance of himself.

Other WRs ranked inside the Top-30 and/or who should be starting options this season and who I'm lower than consensus on this week include Jameson Williams (@MIA, third fewest FPPG allowed to opposing WRs, and he's a bit boom/bust), Michael Wilson (@SEA, with a QB change and going against the #1 fantasy defense vs. WRs) and Tre Tucker (@SF), who is as boom/bust as it gets.

Elite options this week – Brock Bowers, Trey McBride, George Kittle and Isaiah Likely; the analysis starts below them.

Harold Fannin, Jr. (@NO) is coming off a monster game after a mostly quiet start to the season. I think the QB change won't matter much for him, and he'll keep it going against a defense that hasn't covered tight ends well (fifth most FPPG allowed to the position).

Others in the Top-12 this week who I like for strong performances: Tucker Kraft (@NE), Travis Kelce (vs. NYJ), Sam LaPorta to bounce back after two down games (@MIA, third most FPPG allowed to opposing tight ends), and (gulp) Kyle Pitts, Sr. (vs. CIN).

Greg Dulcich (vs. DET) gets the gift that is the Lions' defense, which has allowed the most everything to tight ends. He's also been busy the last few weeks, with at least five targets in four straight games. Miami should need to throw a lot in this contest.

Mike Gesicki (@ATL) is mostly TD or bust, but the TDs keep coming (five straight games). He's there if you need him.

Others to consider if you're in need of a streamer: T.J. Hockenson (@BUF), Brenton Strange (@BAL), Tyler Higbee (@WAS, second most FPPG allowed to the position, but Rams' tight ends are the ultimate roulette wheel), and Mason Taylor (@KC, he's been on the rise and the Chiefs allow the fifth most FPPG to opposing tight ends).

Thank you, Dalton Kincaid (@MIN) for showing up last week! Unfortunately, this week's matchup is brutal. The Vikings have allowed the fewest catches, yards, FPPG, and touchdowns (zero) to opposing tight ends.

Jake Ferguson (@IND), are you still in the NFL? If he plays, he might be limited with an ankle injury.

More Fades: Mark Andrews (vs. JAC), Juwan Johnson (vs. CLE, and after a fast start he has faded fast, with just five catches for 23 yards over the past three games).

That's all I've got this week. Good luck in Week 9!

#2874. Jerry Jeudy delivers strong Shedeur Sanders endorsement ahead of potential Browns QB change

Jerry Jeudy has backed Shedeur Sanders as the Cleveland Browns prepare for a possible quarterback change ahead of their clash with the New Orleans Saints.

Sanders could be in line for his first start of the season with Deshaun Watson in concussion protocol. Although Watson has not been ruled out, Sanders took first-team practice reps as Cleveland prepares for the possibility.

The Browns are 5-3 and will be keen to maintain their momentum. Jeudy believes Sanders has the ability to help them do exactly that.

Asked what gives him confidence in Sanders, Jeudy highlighted the quarterback's ability to make plays downfield.

Speaking to Cleveland.com's Mary Kay Cabot, the receiver said: "Just his competitive nature, man, his big-play ability, the way he drives the ball downfield, gives the receiver chances and just makes plays. He's a baller, man."

Sanders is also familiar with the responsibility of stepping in for Watson. He took over after Dillon Gabriel suffered a concussion last season and started Cleveland's final seven games, finishing that stretch with a 3-4 record.

The second-year quarterback threw seven touchdowns and 10 interceptions during his rookie campaign. However, he has now had a full offseason working in Todd Monken's offence and building relationships with Cleveland's receivers.

Monken has also been impressed by how Sanders has approached his backup role, saying: "Since I've been here, all he's done is work. All he's done is be diligent and ask great questions."

Jeudy's endorsement is particularly notable given their disagreement during Cleveland's 26-8 defeat to the San Francisco 49ers last season. Cameras captured the pair exchanging words while reviewing a play on the sideline, but Jeudy later insisted: "We spoke about it. We're good."

The receiver has had a quieter season, recording 16 receptions for 171 yards and no touchdowns through eight games. He also went without a catch on one target against the Pittsburgh Steelers.

With rookies KC Concepcion and Denzel Boston taking on starting responsibilities, Jeudy has had fewer opportunities. Monken, however, has continued to back the veteran, saying: "I mean, we're better with Jerry than we are without Jerry."

If Sanders gets the nod against New Orleans, the pair will have an opportunity to turn that support into a productive connection on the field.

#2872. Walker Kessler admitted Domantas Sabonis outplayed him before strong fourth quarter

Prior to Wednesday's fourth quarter, Walker Kessler was sitting on five points, three rebounds and two steals — far lesser production than he'd proven capable of thus far in this season. Then, he exploded for a 10-point, five-rebound, four-block, one-steal final frame to finish with 15 points, eight rebounds, four blocks and one steal. Those are numbers much more in line with the 25-year-old's early season signature.

Kessler's counterpart in the middle was Sacramento Kings center Domantas Sabonis. He's a rugged, brawny big man who's unafraid to launch elbows and hips into defenders for space down low and also threads passes to teammates for easy scores. Before that fourth-quarter renaissance, Kessler recognized the Lithuanian veteran — who logged 16 points, 17 rebounds (six on the offensive glass), one assist and one steal — was getting the best of him.

"He's a very, very talented player. Really physical player," Kessler told reporters after the Los Angeles Lakers' 116-103 win on Wednesday. "Definitely got my tail kicked a little bit in the first three quarters. But I relied on my teammates and they did a good job of encouraging me … At the end of the day, just trying to win and started to roll my way a little bit."

While likely past his All-NBA apex at this stage of his career, the 30-year-old Sabonis remains a good player and tough cover. For Kessler to struggle through three quarters but still stay in the fight mentally to bounce back and help fuel a third straight Lakers victory is encouraging. Of course, the white-hot exploits of Luka Dončić, who notched his fourth consecutive 40-point outing, didn't ding Los Angeles' win probability either.

Kessler will face another big, strong body Friday night when Donovan Clingan and the West-leading, 6-1 Portland Trail Blazers come to town. It'll mark both the Lakers' second meeting of the year with Portland and their second NBA Cup Group Play game. After blowing a big lead to the Golden State Warriors last Friday in their opening NBA Cup matchup of 2026-27, they must win this one to maintain any hope of advancing to the Quarterfinals. Tipoff is schedule for 7 p.m.

#2864. Yaxel Lendeborg has changed everything for Steph Curry, Warriors this season

This was supposed to be a wasted season for Steph Curry.

The Warriors were injury-riddled and coming off a lackluster summer in which they made no major moves.

It meant the greatest shooter of all time was destined to tread water as he turned 39 years old.

He has resuscitated Curry's career. He has breathed new life into a flailing dynasty. He has given the Warriors their future franchise star.

Lendeborg was a question mark when the Warriors selected him as the No. 11 pick in the 2026 draft.

Can a jack-of-all-trades turn into a star? Can a 24-year-old surpass expectations or has he already peaked? Can a rookie really be NBA ready?

Lendeborg has emphatically answered those questions.

He's averaging 21.5 points (50% shooting from the field, 40% from 3), seven rebounds and 3.9 assists for the Warriors, who have won five games in a row.

Golden State Warriors' Yaxel Lendeborg dribbles against the Phoenix Suns during an NBA game at Chase Center in San Francisco, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

The Warriors are good. Curry has a wingman. This is thrilling for anyone who loves basketball.

No one seems to be savoring this more than Curry, whose joy has been on full display amid the Warriors' unexpected rise.

During his 44-point deluge against the Timberwolves on Oct. 28, Curry flashed a lunge pose after sinking a shot from beyond the arc.

After his jab sent Luka Doncic onto his butt and he swished a 3-pointer against the Lakers on Oct. 30, he did the Edvard Munch celebration, putting his hands on his cheeks and opening his mouth in shock.

And after scoring 34 points against the Suns on Monday, he didn't hesitate when asked what has changed for the Warriors this season.

"I mean, Yax," Curry said, flashing a smile.

Golden State Warriors guard Steph Curry warms up before a game against the Los Angeles Lakers in Los Angeles, Wednesday, Oct. 21, 2026. London Entertainment

Curry and Lendeborg are the NBA's second-highest scoring duo (51.5 points per game), behind Luka Dončić and Austin Reaves (61.8). Lendeborg's success has been a life raft for the Warriors, who are without Jimmy Butler and Moses Moody for the first few months of the season and without Kristaps Porzingis indefinitely.

In many ways, Lendeborg has unlocked Curry. He has made his brilliance count. He has lit a new fire under him.

Curry, who missed 27 straight games because of runner's knee last season, is averaging 30 points (46.8% shooting from the field, 42.9% from beyond the arc), 4.2 rebounds and 4.5 assists per game.

He just surpassed Michael Jordan (44) for the most 40-point games in NBA history since turning 30, notching his 45th such performance last week.

Curry is playing as though he were a decade younger. And that's because a guy 14 years younger than him is making all of this meaningful.

It's funny because this really couldn't have worked out better for both parties.

Curry, a two-time MVP and four-time champion, is doing what he loves most: winning.

And the outspoken Lendeborg has found a team that needs him to contribute immediately and encourages his bravado, two things that could've been problematic with other franchises.

Yaxel Lendeborg #1 of the Golden State Warriors drives to the basket against Jalen Green #4 of the Phoenix Suns in San Francisco on Nov. 2, 2026. Getty Images

And neither is being politically correct.

The rookie recently raised eyebrows when he stunningly said he lost all respect for Dončić following their trash talk in the season opener. The Warriors didn't wince.

Instead, Curry, Draymond Green and coach Steve Kerr unapologetically had his back.

Lendeborg landed in the perfect situation. He has a mentor in Green, who's famous for unabashedly speaking his mind. And he has a franchise behind him with a win-now mentality that needs him to shine.

Lendeborg has found his home. He can flourish here. He needs to flourish here.

It's in the form of a rookie who has made it so that instead of treading water he's soaring.

#2860. What is Rams’ plan for Ty Simpson?

The Los Angeles Rams had a plan to futureproof the quarterback position. They hid their intentions well during the spring before shocking the NFL by selecting Ty Simpson with the thirteenth overall pick.

But a plan—at least a good one—doesn't stop at drafting a player.

We knew Simpson was a green prospect entering the draft. He looked as ready as you possibly could during the preseason. For someone who the consensus said needed time to develop, Simpson looked poised under pressure and kept the ball out of harm's way during the exhibition games.

Through eight weeks Simpson has been a healthy scratch on game days. He still dressed as the emergency third quarterback behind Matthew Stafford and Stetson Bennett, although he cannot step onto the field unless the two signal callers ahead of him are unavailable because of injury.

It's time to reassess whether Simpson should be the third quarterback. That is not to say that Simpson should be starting over Stafford, although just two weeks ago it was difficult to see LA climbing out of the hole they put themselves in.

I have a hard time believing that Bennett is more capable and dependable than Simpson. It's probably time for the rookie to usurp the veteran within the depth chart. Stafford is also managing an elbow injury that requires him to frequently ice his elbow, which could help explain his decline in play this season.

It's worth mentioning: the Rams owe Bennett nothing. His contract is set to expire at season's end. It would take more money to retain him, which the team probably won't do. The most likely outcome is that Bennett goes to team that runs a similar offensive scheme to Sean McVay and where he has a chance to get onto the field.

So if the Rams are headed towards a separation with Bennett, why not give Simpson any reps as the number two quarterback and allow him to backup Stafford on gamedays?

It could be that the team is waiting for their bye week to make a change. That comes after the next two games.

Still, we know the Rams have a plan to develop Simpson. The details are simply unclear.

At some point the rookie will replace Bennett as the backup. It's really just a matter of when. Why not right now?

#2858. Joe Brady on cutting S C.J. Gardner-Johnson: "It was the best interests of the football team"

On the day the NFL suspended former Bills safety C.J. Gardner-Johnson for spitting on an opponent, Buffalo coach Joe Brady said the team would not be cutting Gardner-Johnson. After the game that Gardner-Johnson missed, the Bills cut him.

Meeting with reporters on Thursday, Brady explained the reversal.

“There was a lot to evaluate,” Brady said. “You know, we got together and figured out, ‘Hey, this is what we felt like this was the best thing to do for the Buffalo Bills.’ I have so much love for [C.J.]. Like, I really do. I loved getting to coach him. I loved being around him. I loved — I did love the edge, and his personality allowing him to be him, right?, and as a person. So that didn’t make the decision easy at all.

“But we also have to be an organization that, you know, it’s unfortunate obviously what happened, you know? But it also opened a door and an opportunity for guys the other day, and that was a part of evaluation. And seeing how those guys kind of played, and you know with a lot of the roster moves. And, again, this wasn’t — we didn’t come out of the game post Vegas and say, ‘This is what we were going to do. We’re just gonna do this the following Monday.’ We were, you know, the plan was him being here and we were going to evaluate it after the game in Baltimore. And we felt like with some of the moving parts we had to do it was the best interests of the football team.”

Gardner-Johnson’s suspension opened the door for Damar Hamlin to start the game against the Ravens. And Hamlin apparently did well enough to help the Bills realize that, all things considered, the “best interests” of the Bills meant the Bills were better off without the “high maintenance” Gardner-Johnson.
